Graded multiplicities in the Kostant-Rallis setting
Andrew Frohmader

Abstract
. This paper contains two main results. First, we provide combinatorial branching rules for GL n ↓ O n \GL_{n}\downarrow\Or_{n} and GL 2 ​ n ↓ Sp 2 ​ n \GL_{2n}\downarrow\Sp_{2n} extending the Littlewood restriction rules. Second, we use these branching rules and the combinatorics of GL n \GL_{n} -crystals to derive a formula for the graded multiplicity of a K K -type in the regular functions on the K K -nilpotent cone for GL ⁡ ( n , ℝ ) \GL(n,\R) , GL ⁡ ( n , ℂ ) \GL(n,\C) and GL ⁡ ( n , ℍ ) \GL(n,\Qu) . Due to work of Schmid and Vilonen, these graded multiplicities determine the Hodge K K -character of the spherical principal series with infinitesimal character 0.



1.
Introduction

Let
G
be a connected complex reductive algebraic group,
G_{\R}
a real form with maximal compact subgroup
K_{\R}
, and
\g_{\R}=\kk_{\R}\oplus\p_{\R}
the Cartan decomposition of its Lie algebra. Let
\theta:G\to G
be the corresponding complexified Cartan involution,
K=G^{\theta}
the group of
\theta
-fixed points,
\g
the Lie algebra of
G
,
\kk=\{X\in\g:d\theta(X)=X\}
the Lie algebra of
K
, and
\p=\{X\in\g:d\theta(X)=-X\}
, so
\g=\kk\oplus\p
.

The adjoint representation of
G
restricts to give a representation of
K
on
\p
. Let
\mathfrak{a}_{\R}
be a maximal abelian subalgebra of
\p_{\R}
and
\mathfrak{a}\subset\p
be its complexification. Let
M
be the centralizer of
\mathfrak{a}
in
K
. Let
\C[\p]
denote the ring of polynomial functions on
\p
,
\C[\p]^{K}
the ring of
K
-invariant polynomials, and
\C[\p]^{K}_{+}
the ideal in
\C[\p]
generated by all elements of
\C[\p]^{K}
without a constant term. Let
\mathcal{N}\subset\g
be the nilpotent cone in
\g
and
\mathcal{N}_{\theta}=\mathcal{N}\cap\p
be the
K
-nilpotent cone. We have,

Theorem 1.1
.

[
KostantRallis1971
, Theorem 14]

The ideal
\C[\p]^{K}_{+}
is the ideal associated to the variety
\mathcal{N}_{\theta}
, i.e.
\C[\p]^{K}_{+}=\{f\in\C[\p]:f(x)=0\text{ for all }x\in\mathcal{N}_{\theta}\}
.

Similarly, let
S(\p)
denote the symmetric algebra over
\p
regarded as a
K
-module,
S(\p)^{K}
the
K
invariant symmetric tensors, and
S(\p)^{K}_{+}
the ideal in
S(\p)^{K}
generated by all elements without a constant term. Each element
v\in S(\p)
is naturally associated with a constant coefficient differential operator
\partial_{v}
on
\p
and a polynomial
f\in\C[\p]
is called
K
-harmonic
if
\partial_{v}f=0
for all
v\in S(\p)^{K}_{+}
. Let
\mathcal{H}\subset\C[\p]
be the subspace of all harmonic polynomials on
\p
. Crucially for us, the spaces
\C[\p]
,
\C[\p]^{K}
, and
\mathcal{H}
are all naturally graded by degree.

Theorem 1.2
.

[
KostantRallis1971
, Theorems 15, 16,17]

The space
\C[\p]
is a free module over the ring of
K
-invariant polynomials, with

\C[\p]=\C[\p]^{K}\otimes\mathcal{H}.

Furthermore, as a representation of
K
,
\mathcal{H}
is equivalent to the representation algebraically induced from the trivial representation of
M
to
K
,

\mathcal{H}\cong\Ind_{M}^{K}1.

Call this setup the
Kostant-Rallis setting
. We draw a couple of conclusions from the two theorems above.

Corollary 1.3
.

As a
K
representation,
\mathcal{H}
is isomorphic to
\C[\mathcal{N}_{\theta}]
and is also isomorphic as a
K
representation to the spherical principal series representations of
G_{\R}
.

We can immediately understand the multiplicity of a
K
-type in
\mathcal{H}
by applying Frobenius reciprocity to
\Ind_{M}^{K}1
. Let
\widehat{K}
denote the set of equivalence classes of finite-dimensional irreducible complex representations of
K
with rational matrix coefficients. For
\nu\in\widehat{K}
, let
\pi^{\nu}_{K}
be a model for
\nu
.

Corollary 1.4
.

The multiplicity of the irreducible
K
representation
\pi^{\nu}_{K}
in
\mathcal{H}
is the dimension of the subspace of
M
-invariant vectors in
\pi^{\nu}_{k}
, i.e.
\dim(\pi^{\nu}_{K})^{M}
.

It is more difficult to get a handle on the graded multiplicities. For any
K
-module
V
and any
\nu\in\widehat{K}
, set
\mult(\pi_{K}^{\nu},V)\coloneqq\dim\Hom_{K}(\pi^{\nu}_{K},V)
. When
V
is graded, we let
\mult_{q}(\pi_{K}^{\nu},V)
denote the
graded multiplicity
. Specializing
q=1
,
\mult_{1}(\pi_{K}^{\nu},V)=\mult(\pi_{K}^{\nu},V)
. Define,

m^{\nu,0}_{(G,K)}(q)\coloneqq\mult_{q}(\pi^{\nu}_{K},\mathcal{H}).

These polynomials will be our primary interest in this paper. The
\nu
denotes the
K
-type and the
0
indicates that we are considering the trivial representation of
M
. We will only consider trivial
M
here, but it would be interesting to consider other representations of
M
in connection with
K
-types in non-spherical principal series, as discussed below.

A large body of combinatorial representation theory literature explores the special case of the Kostant-Rallis setting corresponding to complex Lie groups. Consider
G_{\R}=\GL(n,\C)
,
\Or(n,\C)
, and
\Sp(2n,\C)
as groups over
\R
with
K_{\R}=\Un(n)
,
\Or(n)
, and
\Sp(2n)
respectively. As we will primarily be dealing with complex groups, we denote
\GL(n,\C),\Or(n,\C),\Sp(2n,\C)
by
\GL_{n},\Or_{n},\Sp_{2n}
. Complexifying, we have
(G,K)=(K^{2},K)=(\GL_{n}^{2},\GL_{n})
,
(\Or_{n}^{2},\Or_{n})
, and
(\Sp_{2n}^{2},\Sp_{2n})
, here
K^{2}=K\times K
and more generally,
K^{n}=K\times\dots\times K
. The Cartan involution just exchanges the two factors of
K
. In this case,
\p=\kk
is the Lie algebra of
K
,
M
is the maximal torus of
K
, and the representation of
K
on
\p
is the adjoint representation. Since
M
is the maximal torus, the set of
M
-invariant vectors in Corollary
1.4
is just the zero weight subspace. Hence, in this setting, the multiplicity of a
K
-type
\pi^{\nu}_{K}
in
\C[\p]=\C[\kk]
is the dimension of the zero weight space of
\pi^{\nu}_{K}
. We call this the
Kostant setting
, since it corresponds to
[
Kostant1963
]
.

In the Kostant setting, a lot of work has gone into seeing the graded multiplicity
m^{\nu,0}_{(K^{2},K)}(q)
, which Kostant called
generalized exponents
, in terms of elements of the zero weight subspace. For example, a theorem of Hesselink establishes that
m^{\nu,0}_{(K^{2},K)}(q)
equals the Lusztig q-analogue associated to the zero weight subspace in
\pi^{\nu}_{K}
,
[
Hesselink1980
]
. For more background, see
[
NelsenRam2003
,
LecouveyLenart2020
,
JosephLetzterZelikson2000
]
.

In this paper, we consider
\GL(n,\R)
,
\GL(n,\C)
, and
\GL(n,\Qu)
. The data we need is summarized in the table below, see
[
GoodmanWallach2009
, Section 12.3.2]
. Let
\M_{p,q}
denote the space of complex
p\times q
matrices,
\M_{n}\coloneqq\M_{n,n}
,
\SM_{n}
the
n\times n
symmetric matrices, and
\AM_{n}
the
n\times n
antisymmetric matrices.

G_{\R},K_{\R}

G,K

\p

M

\GL(n,\R),\Or(n)

\GL_{n},\Or_{n}

\SM_{n}

\Or_{1}^{n}

\GL(n,\C)
,
\Un(n)

\GL_{n}^{2},\GL_{n}

\M_{n}

\GL_{1}^{n}

\GL(n,\Qu),\Sp(2n)

\GL_{2n},\Sp_{2n}

\AM_{2n}

\Sp_{2}^{n}

For these cases, we find

Theorem 1.5
.

[Theorem
6.1
below]

m^{\nu,0}_{(G,K)}(q)=\sum_{T\in(\mathcal{T}_{K}^{\nu})_{0}}q^{d(T)},

where
(\mathcal{T}^{\nu}_{K})_{0}
is a subset of semistandard tableaux representing the space of
M
-invariant vectors in the irreducible representation
\pi^{\nu}_{K}
and
d(T)
is a statistic computed using crystal operators.

The
(\GL_{n}^{2},\GL_{n}
), case falls within the Kostant setting and
d(T)
can be seen as a version of the charge statistic on rational
\GL_{n}
-tableaux,
[
LascouxSchutzenberger1978
]
. The other two case are outside the Kostant setting. Although crystals play an important role in proving this theorem, the resulting formula can be computed in a purely combinatorial manner via the signature rule. We include many explicit computations in Section
6
.

The literature on graded multiplicities in the Kostant-Rallis setting is less developed. Kostant and Rallis describe the graded multiplicities of an irreducible representation
\pi^{\nu}_{K}
in
\mathcal{H}
in terms of the eigenvalues of a certain element of
\mathfrak{k}
, see
[
KostantRallis1971
, Theorem 21]
. In
[
WallachWillenbring2000
]
, Wallach and Willenbring obtain formulas similar to Hesselink for some examples including
(\GL_{2n},\Sp_{2n})
,
(\Sor_{2n+2},\Sor_{2n+1})
, and
(E_{6},F_{4})
. Wallach and Willenbring also worked out the example of
(\SL_{4},\Sor_{4})
explicitly and other results in special cases have appeared,
[
JohnsonWallach1977
,
WillenbringVanGroningen2014
]
. Recently, Mason-Brown extended the Hesselink formula from the Kostant setting to the Kostant-Rallis setting
[
Mason-Brown2022
]
. In general, prior to Theorem
1.5
above, explicit combinatorial formulas only existed in a stable range corresponding to the classical restriction rules of Littlewood
[
Littlewood1945
,
Littlewood1944
,
Willenbring2002
,
HoweTanWillenbring2005
,
HoweTanWillenbring2008
]
.

In order to prove the graded multiplicity formula above, we established generalizations of the Littlewood restriction rules valid for all parameters, i.e. there are no stable range constraints. These results build on
[
Kwon2018
,
KwonJang2021
,
LecouveyLenart2020
]
and may be of independent interest. For
\lambda\in\widehat{G}
and
\nu\in\widehat{K}
, let
b^{\lambda}_{\nu}\coloneqq\mult(\pi_{K}^{\nu},\Res^{G}_{K}(\pi^{\lambda}_{G}))
denote the
branching multiplicity
. We will drop
\Res^{G}_{K}
from the notation and simply write
\mult(\pi_{K}^{\nu},\pi^{\lambda}_{G})
since it is clear from the first term we should view the second term as a representation of
K
.

Theorem 1.6

(Theorem
5.6
below)
.

(1)

For
\lambda\in\widehat{\GL}_{2n}
a polynomial representation and
\nu\in\widehat{\Sp}_{2n}
,

\mult(\pi^{\nu}_{\Sp_{2n}},\pi^{\lambda}_{\GL_{2n}})=\sum_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{(1,1)}_{2n}}c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Sp_{2n}).

(2)

For
\lambda\in\widehat{\GL}_{n}
a polynomial representation, and
\nu\in\widehat{\Or}_{n}
,

\mult(\pi^{\nu}_{\Or_{n}},\pi^{\lambda}_{\GL_{n}})=\sum_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{n}^{(2)}}c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Or_{n}).

The integers
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(K)
are a generalization of the standard Littlewood-Richardson coefficients and can be computed via very minor flag conditions on the traditional Littlewood-Richardson tableaux.
\mathcal{P}^{(2)}_{n}
denotes the set of partitions with even rows and length at most
n
.
\mathcal{P}^{(1,1)}_{2n}
denotes the set of partitions with length at most
2n
and even columns. There are a number of examples in Section
5
demonstrating explicit computations, which in our opinion are no worse than the computation of stable branching via the Littlewood restriction rules.

Next, we discuss the connection between the problem of computing graded multiplicities in the Kostant-Rallis setting and the unitary dual problem for real reductive groups. Recently, it has been shown that the unitarity of an irreducible Harish-Chandra module
\pi
with real infinitesimal character can be read off from the associated graded of a certain filtration on
\pi
, called the Hodge filtration, see
[
AdamsTrapaVogan
,
DavisVilonen2025
]
. We will refer to the graded
K
-character of
\text{gr}(\pi)
as the
Hodge
K
-character
,
\chi^{H}
. This Hodge filtration comes from a geometric approach, realizing
\pi
as the global sections of a
K
-equivariant
\mathcal{D}
-module on the flag variety via Beilinson-Bernstein localization and observing that Saito’s theory of mixed Hodge modules can be applied. The base case in this setting is that of the tempered spherical principal series. We have the following,

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
\pi
be the tempered spherical principal series of
G_{\R}
with real infinitesimal character. Then, as a graded
K
-representation, the associated graded of the Hodge filtration on
\pi
is equal to
\mathcal{H}
.

Proof.

Using Corollary
1.3
this is immediate from a result of Schmid and Vilonen. See
[
AdamsTrapaVogan
, Proposition 13.1]
where the result is attributed to Schmid and Vilonen and
[
DavisVilonen2025
, Theorem 1.10]
where a proof of a slightly more general fact is provided.
∎

Hence, for all cases covered in the Kostant-Rallis setting, the Hodge
K
-character of the spherical principal series with infinitesimal character 0 is given by,

\chi^{H}=\sum_{\nu\in\widehat{K}}m^{\nu,0}_{G,K}(q)\chi^{\nu}_{K},

where
\chi^{\nu}_{K}
is the character of
\pi^{\nu}_{K}
. We hope that the more detailed combinatorial description of the Hodge filtration we provide in this base case can help shed additional light on Hodge filtrations and unitarity more generally. We note that, due to Theorem
1.7
, all existing results about graded multiplicities in the Kostant setting provide information about the Hodge filtrations of spherical principal series representations of complex Lie groups.

The results here should extend to the other classical symmetric pairs. It would be interesting to consider if the statistic
d(T)
applied to other
M
-weight spaces of
\pi^{\nu}_{K}
could provide information about the Hodge filtrations of the non-spherical principal series. We say a little more about this towards the end of Section
2.3
below.

Finally, we briefly outline our approach. For any
G
-module
V
, let
\Spec(V)\coloneqq\{\lambda\in\widehat{G}:\mult(\pi_{G}^{\lambda},V)\neq 0\}
denote the
spectrum
of
V
. In the cases we consider, there is a multiplicity free decomposition of
\C[\p]
under the action of
G
, Theorem
2.9
below. Hence, we can write

\chr_{q}(\C[\p])=\sum_{\lambda\in\Spec(\C[\p])}q^{d_{G}^{\lambda}}\chi^{\lambda}_{G}.

where
d_{G}^{\lambda}
is the degree that
\pi^{\lambda}_{G}
appears in the multiplicity-free decomposition of
\C[\p]
and
\chi^{\lambda}_{G}
is the character of
\pi^{\lambda}_{G}
. Branching from
G
down to
K
,

\displaystyle\chr_{q}(\C[\p])

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in\Spec(\C[\p])}q^{d_{G}^{\lambda}}\sum_{\nu\in\widehat{K}}b^{\lambda}_{\nu}\cdot\chi^{\nu}_{K},

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu\in\widehat{K}}\chi^{\nu}_{K}\sum_{\lambda\in\Spec(\C[\p])}q^{d_{G}^{\lambda}}b^{\lambda}_{\nu},

which we recognize as the decomposition
\chr_{q}(\C[\p])=\sum\chi^{\nu}_{K}\mult_{q}(\pi^{\nu}_{K},\C[\p])
. From this, we see

\mult_{q}(\pi^{\nu}_{K},\C[\p])=\sum_{\lambda\in\Spec(\C[\p])}q^{d_{G}^{\lambda}}b^{\lambda}_{\nu}.

By Theorem
1.2
,

\chr_{q}(\C[\p])=\chr_{q}(\C[\p]^{K})\cdot\chr_{q}(\mathcal{H}).

What we really want is
\chr_{q}(\mathcal{H})
. It is easy to write this as

\displaystyle\chr_{q}(\mathcal{H})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\chr_{q}(\C[\p]^{K})}\cdot\chr_{q}(\C[\p])

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\chr_{q}(\C[\p]^{K})}\cdot\sum_{\nu\in\widehat{K}}\chi^{\nu}_{K}\sum_{\lambda\in\Spec(\C[\p])}q^{d_{G}^{\lambda}}b^{\lambda}_{\nu}.

So,

m^{\nu,0}_{(G,K)}(q)=\mult_{q}(\pi^{\nu}_{K},\mathcal{H})=\frac{1}{\chr_{q}(\C[\p]^{K})}\sum_{\lambda\in\Spec(\C[\p])}q^{d_{G}^{\lambda}}b^{\lambda}_{\nu}.

But we would like to realize
m^{\nu,0}_{(G,K)}
more explicitly on a basis for the
M
-invariants,
(\pi^{\nu}_{K})^{M}
, as in Theorem
1.5
above. To achieve this, we take the following approach:

(1)

Recall the structure of
\C[\p]^{K}
.

(2)

Understand
\C[\p]
as a multiplicity free representation of
G
.

(3)

Branch from
G
to
K
.

(4)

Factor out the invariants.

Items (1) and (2) are contained in the next section. (3) is handle in Sections 3-5 and (4) is completed in Section 6.

Acknowledgments.
I would like to thank Jeb Willenbring for many helpful discussions and Jeff Adams for explaining the connection with Hodge filtrations.

2.
Representation theory preliminaries

We begin by establishing some notation for reductive Lie algebras and Lie groups that we will need in our treatment of crystals. Our notation mainly follows
[
GoodmanWallach2009
]
. As we will only need
\GL_{n}
-crystals for the development of the branching rules, in the second subsection we set conventions for
\GL_{n}
. In the third subsection, we recall parameterizations of the irreducible representations of
\GL_{n}
,
\Or_{n}
, and
\Sp_{2n}
by Young diagrams. We also briefly discuss “
M
-polynomials” and connections with non-spherical principal series. In the fourth subsection we discuss the ring of invariants of
\C[\p]
and in the fifth we review some multiplicity free actions of
G
on
\C[\p]
.

2.1.
Reductive Lie algebras

Let
H\subset G
be a maximal torus,
n=\dim H
the rank of
G
, and
\chi(H)
the group of rational characters of
H
. Let
\g=\mathfrak{z}(\g)\oplus\g^{\prime}
be the Lie algebra of
G
, where
\mathfrak{z}(\g)
is the center of
\g
and
\g^{\prime}=[\g,\g]
is semisimple. Fix a Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}_{0}
of
\g^{\prime}
and let
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{h}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{z}(\g)
be a Cartan subalgebra of
\g
such that
\mathfrak{h}=\Lie(H)
. Let
\Phi\subset\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{*}
be the root system of
\g^{\prime}
relative to
\mathfrak{h}_{0}
,
\Phi^{+}
be a subset of positive roots and
\Delta=\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r}\}
the corresponding set of simple roots. Let
r=|\Delta|
be the semisimple rank of
\g
. For each
\alpha\in\Phi
, let
\{e_{\alpha},f_{\alpha},h_{\alpha}\}\subset\g^{\prime}
be the TDS triple spanning a Lie algebra isomorphic to
\sll(2,\C)
.
h_{\alpha}
is the coroot to
\alpha
and
\alpha(h_{\alpha})=2
. For the simple root
\alpha_{i}
, denote the corresponding TDS
\{e_{i},f_{i},h_{i}\}
. Let
X=\{e_{i},f_{i},h_{i}\}_{i=1}^{r}
be the set of standard generators for the semisimple Lie algebra
\g^{\prime}
and extend
X
to a set of generators for
\g
by adding
\{h_{i}\}_{i=r+1}^{n}
elements freely generating
\mathfrak{z}(\g)
.

Let
P(\g)=\{\mu\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}:\mu(h_{\alpha})\in\Z\text{ for all }\alpha\in\Phi\}
be the weight lattice for
\g
. Let
\{\varpi_{1},\dots,\varpi_{r}\}\subset\mathfrak{h}^{*}
be the fundamental weights relative to
\Delta
, i.e.
\varpi_{i}(h_{j})=\delta_{ij}
. In particular,
\varpi_{i}=0
on
\mathfrak{z}(\g)
. Then,
P(\g)=\mathfrak{z}(\g)^{*}\oplus P(\g^{\prime})
, where
P(\g^{\prime})=\{n_{1}\varpi_{1}+\cdots+n_{r}\varpi_{r}:n_{i}\in\Z\}
is the semisimple weight lattice. When
\g=\g^{\prime}
is semisimple,
P(\g)
is a free abelian group of rank
r
having the fundamental weights as basis. If
\mathfrak{z}(\g)\neq 0
, we define additional
\varpi_{i}
for
i=r+1,\dots,n
by the same formula,
\varpi_{i}(h_{j})=\delta_{ij}
. The
\varpi_{i}
are not fundamental weights for
i>r
. The point is that the basis
\{\varpi_{1},\dots,\varpi_{n}\}\subset\mathfrak{h}^{*}
is dual to the extended coroot basis
\{h_{1},\dots,h_{n}\}\subset\mathfrak{h}
and the first
r
elements are coroots and fundamental weights for the semisimple
\g^{\prime}
while the last
n-r
elements are for
\mathfrak{z}(\g)
. For
\mu=m_{1}\varpi_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}\varpi_{n}\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
we define the
semisimple weight map
,
ss(\mu)=m_{1}\varpi_{1}+\cdots+m_{r}\varpi_{r}
projecting
\mu
onto
P(\g^{\prime})
.

Define the dominant integral weights to be
P_{++}(\g)=\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}:\lambda(h_{i})\in\Z_{\geq 0}\text{ for }i\in[r]\}
. We use
[r]
to denote the set
\{1,2,\dots,r\}
. With the above setup, we have
P_{++}(\g)=P_{++}(\g^{\prime})\oplus\mathfrak{z}(\g)=(\Z_{\geq 0}\varpi_{1}+\cdots+\Z_{\geq 0}\varpi_{r})+(\C\varpi_{r+1}+\cdots+\C\varpi_{n})
. Let
P(G)=\{d\psi:\psi\in\chi(H)\}\subset\mathfrak{h}^{*}
be the weight lattice of
G
. Then
P(G)\subset P(\g)
and we set
P_{++}(G)=P(G)\cap P_{++}(\g)
to be the dominant weights for
G
.

Define a partial order
\geq
on
P(\g^{\prime})
by
\lambda\geq\mu
if
\lambda-\mu=\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}\varpi_{i}
where the coefficients
a_{i}
are nonnegative for all
i\in[r]
, i.e.
\lambda-\mu\in P_{++}(\g^{\prime})
. Notice, this is different from the usual partial order
\succeq
on
P(\g^{\prime})
where
\lambda\succeq\mu
if
\lambda-\mu=\sum_{i=1}^{r}b_{i}\alpha_{i}
where the coefficients
b_{i}
are nonnegative for all
i\in[r]
.

For each
\alpha\in\Phi
, let
s_{\alpha}
be the corresponding reflection of
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
, i.e
s_{\alpha}(\beta)=\beta-\beta(h_{\alpha})\alpha
for
\beta\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
and
W
the Weyl group generated by the
s_{\alpha}
. Denote the long Weyl group element
w_{0}
. Recall
w_{0}\Phi^{+}=-\Phi^{+}
and
w_{0}
takes the highest weight of an irreducible finite dimensional representation of
\g
to its lowest weight.

2.2.
Conventions for
\GL_{n}

As discussed in the introduction,
\GL_{n}
denotes the complex general linear group. Similarly,
\SL_{n}
is the complex special linear group and
\gl_{n},\sll_{n}
are their Lie algebras. We take
H\subset\GL_{n}
to be the subgroup of diagonal matrices.
\g=\gl_{n}\cong\C\oplus\sll_{n}
is reductive, not semisimple.
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{h}_{0}+\C I
where
\mathfrak{h}_{0}
consists of diagonal trace zero matrices and
I
is the
n\times n
identity element generating the center of
\gl_{n}
. Let
\epsilon_{i}\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
be the functional
\epsilon_{i}(\text{diag}(a_{1},\dots a_{n}))=a_{i}
, where
\text{diag}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
is the
n\times n
diagonal matrix with entries
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
. Let
E_{i,j}
denote the
n\times n
matrix with one in position
(i,j)
and zeros elsewhere. For
1\leq i<n
, set
h_{i}=E_{i,i}-E_{i+1,i+1}
and
\varpi_{i}=\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+\epsilon_{i}-\frac{i}{n}(\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+\epsilon_{n})
. These are the coroots and fundamental weights of
\sll_{n}
. Define
h_{n}=I
and
\varpi_{n}=\frac{1}{n}(\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+\epsilon_{n})
. The dominant integral weights of
\gl_{n}
are
P_{++}(\gl_{n})=P_{++}(\sll_{n})\oplus\mathfrak{z}(\gl_{n})=\Z_{\geq 0}\varpi_{1}+\cdots+\Z_{\geq 0}\varpi_{n-1}+\C\varpi_{n}
. From this, it is easy to see that
P_{++}(\gl_{n})
consists of all weights
m_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}\epsilon_{n}
with
m_{1}\geq m_{2}\geq\cdots\geq m_{n}
,
m_{i}\in\C
and
m_{i}-m_{i+1}\in\Z
.

The weight lattice for
\GL_{n}
is
P(\GL_{n})=\Z\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+\Z\epsilon_{n}
and from the characterization of
P_{++}(\gl_{n})
above, we see
P_{++}(\GL_{n})=P(\GL_{n})\cap P_{++}(\gl_{n})
consists of all weights

\text{$\mu=m_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}\epsilon_{n}$ with $m_{1}\geq m_{2}\geq\cdots\geq m_{n}$, $m_{i}\in\Z$}.

Define the dominant weights
\gamma_{i}=\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+\epsilon_{i}
for
i\in[n]
. The restriction of
\gamma_{i}
to
\mathfrak{h}_{0}
is
\varpi_{i}
for
i\in[n-1]
and is zero for
\gamma_{n}
. The restriction of
\gamma_{i}
to
\mathfrak{z}(\gl_{n})=\C I
is
i\cdot\varpi_{n}
for
i\in[n]
. Hence, in terms of our extended fundamental weights basis,
\gamma_{i}=\varpi_{i}+i\cdot\varpi_{n}
for
i\in[n-1]
and
\gamma_{n}=n\cdot\varpi_{n}
.

The importance of the
\gamma_{i}
is that the elements of
P_{++}(\GL_{n})
can be written uniquely as

\mu=k_{1}\gamma_{1}+\cdots+k_{n}\gamma_{n},\text{ with $k_{i}\in\Z_{\geq 0}$ for $i\in[n-1]$ and $k_{n}\in\Z$}.

The connection between the coefficients for
\mu
in terms of
\gamma_{i}
and
\epsilon_{i}
is
k_{i}=m_{i}-m_{i+1}
, where we define
m_{n+1}=0
. The restriction of
\mu
to
\mathfrak{h}_{0}
is

\mu_{0}=k_{1}\varpi_{1}+\cdots+k_{n-1}\varpi_{n-1}

and the restriction of
\mu
to
\mathfrak{z}(\gl_{n})
is
(m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n})\varpi_{n}
. The restriction
\mu\mapsto\mu_{0}
is just the semisimple weight map discussed above, and we will also write
ss(\mu)=\mu_{0}
.

Notice that
\varpi_{i}|_{\mathfrak{h}_{0}}=\epsilon_{1}|_{\mathfrak{h}_{0}}+\cdots+\epsilon_{i}|_{\mathfrak{h}_{0}}
, since
-\frac{i}{n}(\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+\epsilon_{n})
is zero on
\mathfrak{h}_{0}
and we can also take
\{\epsilon_{1}|_{\mathfrak{h}_{0}},\dots,\epsilon_{n-1}|_{\mathfrak{h}_{0}}\}
as
\Z
-basis for
P(\sll_{n})
.

The Weyl group
W
is the symmetric group
S_{n}
and the long Weyl group element
w_{0}
acts on
P(\GL_{n})
by
w_{0}(\epsilon_{i})=\epsilon_{n+1-i}
. Hence
w_{0}(\gamma_{i})=\gamma_{n}-\gamma_{n-i}
for
i\in[n-1]
and
w_{0}(\gamma_{n})=\gamma_{n}
. Restricting to
\mathfrak{h}_{0}
,
w_{0}(\varpi_{i})=-\varpi_{n-i}
for
i=[n-1]
.

Theorem 2.1
.

[
GoodmanWallach2009
, Theorem 5.5.22, Theorem 3.2.13]

For
\mu\in P_{++}(\GL_{n})
and
\mu_{0}
as above, there exists a unique irreducible rational representation
\pi^{\mu}_{\GL_{n}}
of highest weight
\mu
such that

(1)

The restriction of
\pi^{\mu}_{\GL_{n}}
to
\SL_{n}
is
\pi^{\mu_{0}}_{\SL_{n}}
, i.e. has highest weight
\mu_{0}
.

(2)

The central elements
zI
of
\GL_{n}
act by
z^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}}
.

(3)

The lowest weight of
\pi^{\mu}_{\GL_{n}}
is
w_{0}(\mu)
and the lowest weight of
\pi^{\mu_{0}}_{\SL_{n}}
is
w_{0}(\mu_{0})
.

(4)

The highest weight of the dual representation
(\pi^{\mu}_{\GL_{n}})^{*}
is
-w_{0}(\mu)
and the highest weight of
(\pi^{\mu_{0}}_{\SL_{n}})^{*}
is
-w_{0}(\mu_{0})
.

Denote the highest weights
-w_{0}(\mu)
and
-w_{0}(\mu_{0})
by
\mu^{*}
and
\mu_{0}^{*}
respectively. We state (3) and (4) in terms of the coordinates
\varpi_{i}
since this will be important in our development of the Littlewood-Richardson rule below.

Lemma 2.2
.

ss
and
w_{0}
commute and for
\mu
and
\mu_{0}
as above,

w_{0}(\mu)=(\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{i})\gamma_{n}-k_{1}\gamma_{n-1}-k_{2}\gamma_{n-2}-\cdots-k_{n-1}\gamma_{1}.

and

ss(w_{0}(\mu))=-k_{1}\varpi_{n-1}-k_{2}\varpi_{n-2}-\cdots-k_{n-1}\varpi_{1}.

Hence,

\mu_{0}^{*}=k_{1}\varpi_{n-1}+k_{2}\varpi_{n-2}+\cdots+k_{n-1}\varpi_{1}.

Proof.

Use
w_{0}(\gamma_{i})=\gamma_{n}-\gamma_{n-i}
and
ss(\gamma_{i})=\varpi_{i}
for
i\in[n-1]
along with
w_{0}(\gamma_{n})=\gamma_{n}
and
ss(\gamma_{n})=0
.
∎

Theorem
2.1
gives a parameterization of the irreducible
rational representations
of
\GL_{n}
. The irreducible
polynomial representations
are the subset with highest weight
\mu=m_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}\epsilon_{n}
where
m_{1}\geq\cdots\geq m_{n}\geq 0
equivalently
\mu=k_{1}\gamma_{1}+\cdots+k_{n}\gamma_{n}
with
k_{n}\geq 0
. For
\GL_{n}
, we extend
\geq
to
P(\GL_{n})
by
\lambda\geq\mu
if
\lambda-\mu
parametrizes a polynomial representation, i.e.
\lambda-\mu=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\gamma_{i}
with
a_{i}\geq 0
for all
i\in[n]
. Equivalently,
\lambda-\mu=\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}\epsilon_{i}
with
b_{1}\geq b_{2}\geq\cdots\geq b_{n}\geq 0
.

2.3.
Parameterization of representations and
M
-polynomials

In order to provide concrete multiplicity formulas for
K
-types, we need an explicit parametrization of the irreducible representations of
K
. We recall one way to do this for
K=\GL_{n}
,
\Or_{n}
, and
\Sp_{2n}
using Young diagrams. We also give a parametrization of the irreducible representations of
M
for the cases we are considering.

A
partition
of
k\in\Z_{\geq 0}
is a tuple of nonnegative integers
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots,\lambda_{n})
such that
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n}\geq 0
and
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}=k
. We write
|\lambda|=k
when
\lambda
is a partition of
k
. The
length
of
\lambda
is the largest
l
such that
\lambda_{l}\neq 0
. Write
\ell(\lambda)=l
. We call the total number of
\lambda_{i}
the
ambient length
and write
\ell_{a}(\lambda)=n
. Notice
\ell(\lambda)\leq\ell_{a}(\lambda)
. This ambient length is important for us. We do not suppress trailing
0
’s. By the discussion above, we see the irreducible polynomial representations of
\GL_{n}
are parametrized by partitions of ambient length
n
. We just take the coefficients of the highest weight
\lambda
with respect to the
\epsilon_{i}
basis:
\lambda=\lambda_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{n}\epsilon_{n}\longleftrightarrow(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})
. Similarly, the irreducible rational representations of
\SL_{n}
are parametrized by partitions of ambient length
n-1
:
\lambda_{0}=\lambda_{1}\epsilon_{1}|_{\mathfrak{h}_{0}}+\cdots+\lambda_{n-1}\epsilon_{n-1}|_{\mathfrak{h}_{0}}\longleftrightarrow(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n-1})
.

The
Young diagram
of a partition
\lambda
is an array of boxes sitting in the lower left or right quadrant of
\R^{2}
where the
i^{\text{th}}
row has
\lambda_{i}
boxes. We choose coordinates on
\R^{2}
with
y
negative above the
x
-axis and positive below it and view the
i^{\text{th}}
row of
\lambda
as sitting between
i-1
and
i
, see Example
2.3
below.

We think of partitions as Young diagrams and do not use separate notation to distinguish them. Hence irreducible polynomial representations of
\GL_{n}
are in one-to-one correspondence with Young diagrams of ambient length
n
. Restricting to
\mathfrak{h}_{0}
, Young diagrams of ambient length
n-1
are in one-to-one correspondence with irreducible representations of
\SL_{n}
.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{n}
denote the set of all partitions of ambient length
n
. By the above discussion, this set parametrizes the polynomial irreducible representations of
\GL_{n}
. On
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, the partial order
\geq
is given by
\lambda\geq\mu
if
\lambda-\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, where subtraction is defined by viewing the partition as a tuple of integers in
\Z^{n}
. Let
\mathcal{P}^{(2)}_{n}
be those partitions of ambient length
n
with even rows and
\mathcal{P}^{(1,1)}_{2n}
be those partitions of ambient length
2n
with even columns. Notice
\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{(2)}
, and
\mathcal{P}_{2n}^{(1,1)}
are additive monoids.

The
conjugate
diagram
\lambda^{\prime}
is obtained by flipping the diagram over its main diagonal. If the Young diagram of
\mu
has the same ambient length as
\lambda
and is contained in
\lambda
we write
\lambda\supseteq\mu
. The
skew diagram

\lambda/\mu
is the diagram obtained by removing
\mu
from
\lambda
. A Young diagram
\lambda
can be identify with the skew diagram
\lambda/\varnothing
. Finally, let
\lambda^{\pi}
be the skew-diagram obtained by rotating
\lambda
by
180^{\circ}
about the origin and translating vertically by
\ell_{a}(\lambda)+1=n+1
(downward translation). This has the affect of reversing the order of the partition
\lambda
, i.e.
\lambda^{\pi}=(\lambda_{n},\lambda_{n-1},\dots,\lambda_{1})
and is in general no longer a partition since
\lambda_{n}\leq\lambda_{n-1}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{1}
.

to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$4$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont \cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$5$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont\cr}}\kern 2070.4pt}=(0,1,3,3,5)
.

Notice
\lambda^{\pi}
corresponds to the unique lowest weight of
\pi^{\lambda}_{\GL_{n}}
.

Lemma 2.4
.

\lambda^{\pi}=w_{0}(\lambda).
Hence the
\lambda^{\pi}
with ambient length
n
provide another parametrization of the irreducible polynomial representations of
\GL_{n}
.

Proof.

Write
\lambda=\lambda_{1}\varepsilon_{1}+\dots+\lambda_{n}\varepsilon_{n}
.
w_{0}(\lambda)=\lambda_{1}\epsilon_{n}+\lambda_{2}\epsilon_{n-1}+\cdots+\lambda_{n}\epsilon_{1}
which corresponds to
(\lambda_{n},\lambda_{n-1},\cdots,\lambda_{1})
.
∎

Outside of the polynomial representations of
\GL_{n}
, we do not need a lowest weight parametrization and use the common parameterization of
\widehat{K}
given in the following table:

K

\widehat{K}

\Or_{n}

{
\lambda
: first two columns of
\lambda
contain
\leq n
boxes}

\GL_{n}

{
\lambda=(\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})
:
\ell(\lambda^{+})+\ell(\lambda^{-})\leq n\}

\Sp_{2n}

{
\lambda
:
\ell(\lambda)\leq n
}

If
K=\GL_{n}
and
\lambda^{+}=(\lambda^{+}_{1},\dots,\lambda_{i}^{+}),\lambda^{-}=(\lambda_{1}^{-},\dots,\lambda_{j}^{-})
, then
\pi^{\lambda}_{\GL_{n}}
is the irreducible representation with highest weight
(\lambda_{1}^{+},\lambda_{2}^{+},\dots,-\lambda_{2}^{-},-\lambda_{1}^{-})
, see
[
STEMBRIDGE1987
, Proposition 2.1]
. In particular, the polynomial representations are given by
\lambda=(\lambda^{+},\varnothing)
. In this case, we will suppress the
\varnothing
and think of
\lambda
as a Young diagram as above. For
\Sp_{2n}
,
\pi^{\lambda}_{\Sp_{2n}}
is just the irreducible representation with highest weight
\lambda
in standard coordinates. For
K=\Or_{n}
, which is not connected, highest weights do not directly apply, see
[
GoodmanWallach2009
, 438 -439]
. Recall, the irreducible representations of
\Or_{n}
and
\Sp_{2n}
are self-dual, while the irreducible representations of
\GL_{n}
are not. For
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
, the diagram associated to
(\pi^{\lambda}_{\GL_{n}})^{*}
is
\lambda^{*}=(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1})
. Notice, for a polynomial representation given by
\lambda=(\lambda^{+},\varnothing)
,
\lambda^{*}=(\varnothing,\lambda^{+})
is not polynomial unless
\lambda^{+}=\varnothing
.

Next we want to fill these diagrams in a way that will allow us to easily compute the
M
-type multiplicities in
\Res^{K}_{M}(\pi^{\lambda}_{K})
. The way of doing this for polynomial
\GL_{n}
irreducible representations in the context of the symmetric pair
(\GL_{n}^{2},\GL_{n})
is by semistandard Young tableaux. Recall a
(semistandard Young) tableau
is a filling of
\lambda
that is

(1)

weakly increasing across each row,

(2)

strictly increasing down each column.

More generally, a
skew-tableau
on
\lambda/\mu
is a filling satisfying (1) and (2). Denote the set of skew-tableau on shape
\lambda/\mu
with fillings in the alphabet
[n]
by
\SST^{\lambda/\mu}_{n}
and the Young tableaux on
\lambda
with fillings in
[n]
by
\SST^{\lambda}_{n}
. For every
\lambda
with ambient length
n
, let
s^{\lambda}_{n}
be the associated
Schur polynomial
,

s^{\lambda}_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\sum_{T\in\SST^{\lambda}_{n}}x^{\wt(T)}.

Here
\wt(T)=w_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+w_{n}\epsilon_{n}
where
w_{i}=\text{number of $i$'s in $T$}
, and
x^{\wt(T)}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{w_{i}}
. The Schur polynomial
s^{\lambda}_{n}
is the character of
\pi^{\lambda}_{\GL_{n}}
. We continue to denote the diagonal maximal torus in
\GL_{n}
by
H
. Notice, the coefficient of
x^{\wt(T)}
is the multiplicity of the torus representation
\pi^{\wt(T)}_{H}
in
\Res^{\GL_{n}}_{H}(\pi^{\lambda}_{\GL_{n}})
, i.e. the
\wt(T)
weight space. For the symmetric pair
(\GL_{n}^{2},\GL_{n})
,
M=H
and the semistandard tableaux are exactly capturing the multiplicities in the branching problem from
K
to
M
for polynomial representations.
Definition
2.5
characterizes the fillings that have the same property in the three cases we consider. Before presenting the definition, we give a parametrization of the
\widehat{M}
in the relevant cases.

The irreducible representations
\pi^{\delta}_{M}
of
M
are labeled by the following
n
-tuples
\delta
:

\begin{array}[]{l|l|l}K&M&\delta\in\widehat{M}\\
\hline\cr\Or_{n}&\Or_{1}^{n}&\delta\in\{0,1\}^{n}\\
\GL_{n}&\GL_{1}^{n}&\delta\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\\
\Sp_{2n}&\Sp_{2}^{n}&\delta\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}\end{array}

In the case of
\Or_{1}
, 0 denotes the trivial representation and 1 denotes the alternating representation.
\GL_{1}^{n}
is just the maximal torus in
\GL_{n}
and
\delta=(\delta_{1},\dots\delta_{n})\in\Z^{n}
denotes the character with weight
\delta_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+\delta_{n}\epsilon_{n}
. For
\Sp_{2}
,
m\in\Z_{\geq 0}
parametrizes the representation with highest weight
m\varpi_{1}=m\epsilon_{1}
.

Definition 2.5
.

K
-tableaux and their
M
-weights

(1)

A tableau
T\in\SST^{\lambda}_{n}
is an
\Or_{n}
-tableau
if, for all
1\leq i\leq n
, we have

\#\{\text{boxes in first two columns of $T$ with entry $\leq i$}\}\leq i.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{\Or_{n}}^{\lambda}
denote the set of
\Or_{n}
-tableaux on shape
\lambda
. We define
\mwt_{\Or_{n}}(T)
to be the vector
(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n})\in\widehat{M}=\{0,1\}^{n}
such that

w_{i}=\#\{\text{boxes in $T$ with entry $i$}\}\>{\rm mod}\>2.

(2)

A pair
(T^{+},T^{-})\in\SST^{\lambda^{+}}_{n}\times\SST^{\lambda^{-}}_{n}
is a
\GL_{n}
-tableau
if, for all
1\leq i\leq n
, we have

\#\{\text{boxes in first column of $T^{+}$ or $T^{-}$ with entry $\leq i$}\}\leq i.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{\GL_{n}}^{\lambda}
denote the set of
\GL_{n}
-tableaux on shape
\lambda
. We define
\mwt_{\GL_{n}}(T^{+},T^{-})
to be the vector
(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n})\in\widehat{M}=\mathbb{Z}^{n}
such that

w_{i}=\#\{\text{boxes in $T^{+}$ with entry $i$}\}-\#\{\text{boxes in $T^{-}$ with entry $i$}\}.

(3)

A tableau
T\in\SST^{\lambda}_{2n}
is an
\Sp_{2n}
-tableau
if, for all
1\leq i\leq n
, we have

\#\{\text{boxes in first column of $T$ with entry $\leq 2i$}\}\leq i.

Moreover, we say that
T
is an
\Sp_{2n}
-ballot tableau
if, as one reads the word of
T
right to left, the number of
2i
’s never exceeds the number of
2i-1
’s, for each
1\leq i\leq n
.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{\Sp_{2n}}^{\lambda,H}
denote the set of
\Sp_{2n}
-tableaux on shape
\lambda
and
\mathcal{T}_{\Sp_{2n}}^{\lambda}
be the set of
\Sp_{2n}
-ballot tableaux on
\lambda
. If
T
is an
\Sp_{2n}
-ballot tableau, then we define
\mwt_{\Sp_{2n}}(T)
to be the vector
(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n})\in\widehat{M}=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}
such that

w_{i}=\#\{\text{boxes in $T$ with entry $2i-1$}\}-\#\{\text{boxes in $T$ with entry $2i$}\}.

Note that Definition
2.5
can be restated as follows:
T
is a
K
-tableau if and only if, upon restricting
T
to those entries
\leq i
(or
\leq 2i
for
\Sp_{2n}
), the resulting shape lies in the set
\widehat{\Or}_{i}
or
\widehat{\GL}_{i}
or
\widehat{\Sp}_{2i}
. Call the shape resulting from only looking at boxes with filling
\leq i
the shape
supported
on
[i]
. Then the condition for
T
to be a
K_{n}
-tableau is that the shape supported on
[i]
(or
[2i]
for
K_{n}=\Sp_{2n}
) is a
K_{i}
-tableau for all
i
. Also, observe that the set
\mathcal{T}^{\lambda}_{K}
is empty unless
\lambda\in\widehat{K}
. Finally, an equivalent characterization of the
\Sp_{2n}
-tableaux is that the entries in row
i
are at least
2i-1
for
i\in[n]
.

Note, the order preserving bijection
i\mapsto 2i-1
,
\overline{\iota}\mapsto 2i
gives a bijection between the
\Sp_{2n}
-tableaux used here and those in
[
CEFW2025
]
.

The tableaux in
\mathcal{T}^{\lambda}_{K}
give a parameterization of the
M
irreducible representations in
\Res^{K}_{M}(\pi^{\lambda}_{K})
. In the case of
\Or_{n}
and
\GL_{n}
,
M
is abelian so all irreducible representations are 1-dimensional and
|\mathcal{T}^{\lambda}_{K}|=\dim\pi^{\lambda}_{K}
. For
\Sp_{2n}
,
M
is a product of
\Sp_{2}
. Here the
M
irreducible representations are not 1-dimensional unless
\delta=(0,\dots,0)
, and thus
|\mathcal{T}^{\lambda}_{\Sp_{2n}}|\neq\dim\pi^{\lambda}_{\Sp_{2n}}
in general. The full set of
\Sp_{2n}
-tableaux,
\mathcal{T}^{\lambda,H}_{\Sp_{2n}}
come from branching down to a maximal torus
H
, hence the notation. They parameterize a weight basis for
\pi^{\lambda}_{\Sp_{2n}}
. The ballot tableau come from branching to
M
. They parameterize the highest weights of the
M=\Sp_{2}^{n}
irreducible representations occurring
\Res^{\Sp_{2n}}_{\Sp_{2}^{n}}(\pi^{\lambda}_{\Sp_{2n}})
.

Example 2.6
.

We illustrate the difference between
\Sp_{4}
-tableaux and
\Sp_{4}
-ballot tableaux for
\lambda=(2,1)=\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}
.

There are 4
\Sp_{4}
-ballot tableau and 16
\Sp_{4}
-tableaux. The ballot tableaux are,

\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$1$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$3$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$4$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt},\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$3$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$3$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$4$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}.

These are the highest weights for four
\Sp_{2}^{2}
irreducible representations of highest weights
2\varpi_{1}+\varpi_{2}
,
\varpi_{1}+2\varpi_{2}
,
\varpi_{1}
, and
\varpi_{2}
. The first two are 6 dimensional, and the last two are 2 dimensional. It is easy to find the remaining
\Sp_{4}
-tableaux.

Definition 2.7
.

Denote the subset of
\mathcal{T}^{\lambda}_{K}
with
M
-weight
\delta
by
(\mathcal{T}^{\lambda}_{K})_{\delta}
.

The following result of
[
CEFW2025
]
formalizes the discussion above. Recall, for
\lambda\in\widehat{K}
and
\delta\in\widehat{M}
,
b^{\lambda}_{\delta}=\mult(\pi^{\delta}_{M},\pi^{\lambda}_{K})
.

Theorem 2.8
.

[
CEFW2025
, Theorem 4.2]

Let
\lambda\in\widehat{K}
and
\delta\in\widehat{M}
.

b^{\lambda}_{\delta}=\#(\mathcal{T}^{\lambda}_{K})_{\delta}.

See Section
6
for examples. A nice way to organize this information is in what we call the
M
-polynomial
,

M_{(G,K)}^{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}^{\lambda}_{K}}x^{\mwt_{K}(T)},

where
x^{\mwt_{K}(T)}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{w_{i}}
. The coefficient of
x^{\delta}
is
b^{\lambda}_{\delta}
. By Frobenius reciprocity,
b^{\lambda}_{\delta}
is the multiplicity of
\pi^{\lambda}_{K}
in the principal series representations induced from
\delta
. So
M_{(G,K)}^{\lambda}
encodes the multiplicity of
\pi^{\lambda}_{K}
in all the principal series representations of
G_{\R}
. In the case of
G_{\R}=\GL_{n}
,
M^{\lambda}_{(\GL_{n}^{2},\GL_{n})}=s^{\lambda}_{n}
, the (rational) Schur polynomial, see
[
STEMBRIDGE1987
]
.

This suggests we could look for a
graded
M
-polynomial
,
M^{\lambda}_{(G,K)}(x,q)
, a
q
-analogue of the
M
-polynomial where the power of
q
tracks the degree
\pi^{\lambda}_{K}
appears in the associated graded of the Hodge filtration of the principal series with infinitesimal character 0. Viewing
\lambda
as a parameter, we have
M_{(G,K)}(x,q)
. Write

M_{(G,K)}(x,q)=\sum_{\delta\in\widehat{M}}x^{\delta}\sum_{\nu\in\widehat{K}}m^{\nu,\delta}_{(G,K)}(q)\chi^{\nu}_{K},

where
m^{\nu,\delta}_{(G,K)}(q)
gives the graded multiplicity of
\pi^{\nu}_{K}
in the associated graded of the Hodge filtration on the principal series induced from
\delta
with infinitesimal character 0. Taking the coefficient of
x^{\delta}
in
M_{(G,K)}
gives the Hodge
K
-character of principal series representation with infinitesimal character 0 induced from
\delta
. From this perspective, what this paper accomplishes is an explicit computation of the coefficient of
x^{0}=1
. It would be interesting to see if similar statistics can be used to compute all of
M_{(G,K)}(x,q)
.

2.4.
The ring of invariants
\C[\p]^{K}

The well-known work of Chevalley establishes the structure of
\C[\p]^{K}
. In all cases, this is a polynomial ring, i.e. generated by algebraically independent elements. In the three cases we consider,
\C[\p]^{K}\cong\C[u_{1},\dots,u_{n}]
where
u_{i}(X)=\tr(X^{i})
. Hence, for
(\GL_{n},\Or_{n})
,
(\GL_{n}^{2},\GL_{n})
, and
(\GL_{2n},\Sp_{2n})
the graded character of the invariants is,

\chr_{q}(\C[\p]^{K})=\frac{1}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}(1-q^{i})}.

For more detail, see
[
GoodmanWallach2009
, Section 12.4.2]
.

2.5.
\C[\p]
as a multiplicity-free space

In the cases we consider, there are well-known multiplicity-free decompositions of
\C[\p]
as a
G
representation which restrict to the representation of
K
on
\C[\p]
, see
[
Howe1995
, Section 5.1]
.

Theorem 2.9
.

We have the following multiplicity-free decompositions under the action of
G
,

G

\C[\p]

degree

\GL_{n}

\displaystyle\C[\SM_{n}]=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{n}^{(2)}}\pi_{\GL_{n}}^{\lambda}

|\lambda|/2

\GL_{n}^{2}

\displaystyle\C[\M_{n}]=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\pi^{[\lambda^{*},\lambda]}_{\GL_{n}^{2}}

|\lambda|

\GL_{2n}

\displaystyle\C[\AM_{n}]=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{n}^{(1,1)}}\pi_{\GL_{n}}^{\lambda}

|\lambda|/2

where
\pi^{[\lambda^{*},\lambda]}_{\GL_{n}^{2}}
denotes the
\GL_{n}^{2}
irreducible representation
(\pi_{\GL_{n}}^{\lambda})^{*}\boxtimes\pi_{\GL_{n}}^{\lambda}
. The representation
\pi^{\lambda}_{G}
occurs in degree shown in the third column.

Proof.

These are theorems 5.6.7, 5.7.3, and 5.7.5 in
[
GoodmanWallach2009
]
. See also
[
Howe1995
]
.
∎

3.
Crystals of reductive Lie algebras

We give a short review of the facts we need about crystals. For more, see
[
BumpSchilling2017
,
HongKang2002
]
. Our conventions follow
[
BumpSchilling2017
]
. In this paper, the point of crystals is to provide a combinatorial model for finite dimensional representations of complex reductive Lie algebras. Crystals originally arose independently as limits/crystallizations of representations of quantum groups on the one hand and out of standard monomial theory and Littelmann paths on the other. Bump and Schilling
[
BumpSchilling2017
]
provide a purely combinatorial treatment relying on character theory to make the connection with Lie algebras. Much of this can be done in the generality of Kac-Moody algebras as in
[
HongKang2002
]
but we will not need that here. Let
\g
be a complex reductive Lie algebra and carry forward the notation from Section
2.1
.

In Section
3.1
, we define crystals and other basic notions. In Section
3.2
, we discuss tensor products of crystals and characterize highest and lowest weights in a tensor product. In Section
3.3
, we briefly review normal crystals and how they mirror the finite dimensional representation theory of Lie algebras. Finally, in Section
3.4
, we introduce crystal anti-isomorphisms, a slight generalization of crystal involutions, and show how they interact with tensor products. Our exposition is for crystals of reductive Lie algebras, not just semisimple Lie algebras. We try to put highest and lowest weights on equal footing and use anti-isomorphisms to move between highest and lowest weights.

3.1.
Definitions

We begin with the definition of a finite type crystal. Any crystal mentioned in this paper is finite type. In fact, we will only work with seminormal crystals as defined below.

Definition 3.1
.

A
(finite type) crystal
of
\g
is a nonempty set
\mathcal{B}
together with maps

\displaystyle e_{i},f_{i}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}\sqcup\{0\},

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{i},\varphi_{i}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{B}\to\Z,

\displaystyle\wt

\displaystyle:\mathcal{B}\to P(\g),

for all
i\in[r]
. The maps must satisfy the following conditions,

(1)

If
x,y\in\mathcal{B}
, then
e_{i}(x)=y
if and only if
f_{i}(y)=x
. In this case,

•

\wt(e_{i}x)=\wt(x)+\alpha_{i}
and hence
\wt(f_{i}y)=\wt(y)-\alpha_{i}
.

•

\varepsilon_{i}(e_{i}x)=\varepsilon_{i}(x)-1
and hence
\varepsilon_{i}(f_{i}y)=\varepsilon_{i}(y)+1
.

•

\varphi_{i}(e_{i}x)=\varphi_{i}(x)+1
and hence
\varphi_{i}(f_{i}y)=\varphi_{i}(y)-1
.

(2)

\varphi_{i}(x)=\langle\wt(x),h_{i}\rangle+\varepsilon_{i}(x)
for all
x\in\mathcal{B}
and
i\in[r]
.

The map
\wt
is the
weight map
and the maps
e_{i},f_{i}
are the
Kashiwara operators
,
(raising/lowering operators)
.
\mathcal{B}
is called
seminormal
if

\varphi_{i}(x)=\max\{k\in\Z_{\geq 0}:f_{i}^{k}(x)\neq 0\}\text{ and }\varepsilon_{i}(x)=\max\{k\in\Z_{\geq 0}:e_{i}^{k}(x)\neq 0\},

for all
i\in[r]
. All crystals we consider will be seminormal.
\mathcal{B}
should be thought of as a weight basis for a representation of
\g
,
e_{i},f_{i}
as the elements in the TDS corresponding to simple root
\alpha_{i}
, and (2) a natural condition present in any
\sll_{2}
-string.

For
x\in\mathcal{B}
let

\displaystyle\varphi(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\varphi_{i}(x)\varpi_{i},

\displaystyle\varepsilon(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\varepsilon_{i}(x)\varpi_{i}.

Notice
\varphi(x)
and
\varepsilon(x)\in P_{++}(\g^{\prime})
are dominant semisimple weights. Starting in Section
4
, all crystals will be
\gl_{n}
-crystals. At times, it will be important to separate the weight coming from the semisimple
\sll_{n}
from the weight coming from the action of the center. To this end, we make the following general definitions analogous to the definitions made in Section
2.1
for reductive Lie algebras. Let
x\in\mathcal{B}
. Define the
semisimple weight map

\wt_{ss}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\langle\wt(x),h_{i}\rangle\varpi_{i}

and the
central weight map

\wt_{c}(x)=\sum_{i=r+1}^{n}\langle\wt(x),h_{i}\rangle\varpi_{i}.

Lemma 3.2
.

If
\mathcal{B}
is a crystal for a complex reductive Lie algebra
\g
, then
\wt(x)=\wt_{ss}(x)+\wt_{c}(x)
for all
x\in\mathcal{B}
. If
\g
is semisimple,
\wt(x)=\wt_{ss}(x)
.

Proof.

We are just extracting the coefficients of
\wt(x)
written in terms of the basis
\varpi_{1},\dots,\varpi_{n}
. If
\g
is semisimple,
r=n
and
\wt_{c}(x)=0
.
∎

The
crystal graph
of
\mathcal{B}
is the directed graph with vertices in
\mathcal{B}
and labeled edges
x\xrightarrow{i}y
if
f_{i}(x)=y
. An element
x\in\mathcal{B}
such that
e_{i}(x)=0
for all
i\in[r]
is called a
highest weight element
and in this case the weight of
x
is called a
highest weight
. Similarly, an element
x\in\mathcal{B}
such that
f_{i}(x)=0
for all
i\in[r]
is called a
lowest weight element
and in this case the weight of
x
is called a
lowest weight
.

If
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
are two crystals associated with the same Lie algebra
\g
, a
crystal morphism
is a map
\psi:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{C}\sqcup\{0\}
such that

(1)

if
x\in\mathcal{B}
and
\psi(x)\in\mathcal{C}
, then

(a)

\wt(\psi(x))=\wt(x)
,

(b)

\varepsilon_{i}(\psi(x))=\varepsilon_{i}(x)\text{ for all $i\in[r]$}
,

(c)

\varphi_{i}(\psi(x))=\varphi_{i}(x)\text{ for all $i\in[r]$}
,

(2)

if
x,e_{i}x\in\mathcal{B}
and
\psi(x),\psi(e_{i}x)\in\mathcal{C}
, then
\psi(e_{i}x)=e_{i}\psi(x)
,

(3)

if
x,f_{i}x\in\mathcal{B}
and
\psi(x),\psi(f_{i}x)\in\mathcal{C}
, then
\psi(f_{i}x)=f_{i}\psi(x)
.

A morphism
\psi
is
strict
if it commutes with
e_{i}
and
f_{i}
for all
i\in[r]
and is a
crystal isomorphism
if
\psi
is a bijection with
\psi^{-1}
also a crystal morphism.

3.2.
Tensor products of crystals

If
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
are two crystals associated to the same Lie algebra
\g
, the
tensor product

\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C}
is defined to be the Cartesian product of
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
as a set, with ordered pairs denoted
x\otimes y
. The maps behave as follows,

\displaystyle\wt(x\otimes y)

\displaystyle=\wt(x)+\wt(y),

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}(x\otimes y)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{i}(x)+\max(0,\varphi_{i}(y)-\varepsilon_{i}(x)),

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{i}(x\otimes y)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{i}(y)+\max(0,\varepsilon_{i}(x)-\varphi_{i}(y)),

\displaystyle f_{i}(x\otimes y)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}f_{i}(x)\otimes y\quad\text{if }\varepsilon_{i}(x)\geq\varphi_{i}(y),\\
x\otimes f_{i}(y)\quad\text{if }\varepsilon_{i}(x)<\varphi_{i}(y),\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle e_{i}(x\otimes y)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}e_{i}(x)\otimes y\quad\text{if }\varepsilon_{i}(x)>\varphi_{i}(y),\\
x\otimes e_{i}(y)\quad\text{if }\varepsilon_{i}(x)\leq\varphi_{i}(y).\\
\end{cases}

This is known as the
tensor product rule
. We provide a characterization of highest and lowest weight elements in tensor products.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
be seminormal crystals.

(1)

x\otimes y\in\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C}
is a highest weight element if and only if
y
is a highest weight element in
\mathcal{C}
and
\varepsilon(x)\leq\wt_{ss}(y)
.

(2)

x\otimes y\in\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C}
is a lowest weight element if and only if
x
is a lowest weight element in
\mathcal{B}
and
-\wt_{ss}(x)\geq\varphi(y)
.

Proof.

(1) By the seminormal assumption,
x\otimes y
is a highest weight element if and only if
\varepsilon_{i}(x\otimes y)=0
for all
i\in[r]
. By the tensor product rule, this occurs if and only if
\varepsilon_{i}(y)=0
and
\varepsilon_{i}(x)\leq\varphi_{i}(y)
for all
i\in[r]
. Now
\varphi_{i}(y)=\langle\wt(y),h_{i}\rangle+\varepsilon_{i}(y)=\langle\wt(y),h_{i}\rangle
by the crystal axioms and fact that
y
is a highest weight element. Thus
x\otimes y
is a highest weight element if and only if
y
is a highest weight and
\varepsilon_{i}(x)\leq\langle\wt(y),h_{i}\rangle
for all
i\in[r]
. Now,
\varepsilon_{i}(x)\leq\langle\wt(y),h_{i}\rangle
for all
i\in[r]
if and only if
\varepsilon(x)\leq\wt_{ss}(y)
and the result follows.

(2) The idea is the same as (1).
x\otimes y
is a lowest weight element if and only if
\varphi_{i}(x\otimes y)=0
for all
i\in[r]
if and only if
\varphi_{i}(x)=0
and
\varphi_{i}(y)\leq\varepsilon_{i}(x)
. With
x
a lowest weight,
\varepsilon_{i}(x)=-\langle\wt(x),h_{i}\rangle
and the result follows.
∎

We have the following generalization of the tensor product rule to
k
-fold tensor products,

Lemma 3.4

(
[
BumpSchilling2017
]
Lemma 2.33)
.

Let
x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{B}_{k}
for
\g
-crystals
\mathcal{B}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{B}_{k}
. Then,

\varphi_{i}(x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k})=\max_{j=1}^{k}\bigg(\sum_{h=1}^{j}\varphi_{i}(x_{h})-\sum_{h=1}^{j-1}\varepsilon_{i}(x_{h})\bigg),

and if
j
is the first value where the maximum is attained, then

f_{i}(x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k})=x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{i}(x_{j})\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k}.

Similarly, for
x_{k}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{k}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{B}_{1}
,

\varepsilon_{i}(x_{k}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{1})=\max_{j=1}^{k}\bigg(\sum_{h=1}^{j}\varepsilon_{i}(x_{h})-\sum_{h=1}^{j-1}\varphi_{i}(x_{h})\bigg),

and if
j
is the first value where the maximum is attained, then

e_{i}(x_{k}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{1})=x_{k}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{i}(x_{j})\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{1}.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{1}
and
\mathcal{B}_{2}
be seminormal crystals of complex reductive Lie algebras
\g_{1}
and
\g_{2}
with semisimple ranks
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
. Let
e_{i}^{1},f_{i}^{1},\varphi_{i}^{1},\varepsilon_{i}^{1},\wt^{1}
and
e_{i}^{2},f_{i}^{2},\varphi_{i}^{2},\varepsilon_{i}^{2},\wt^{2}
denote their operators. Reindex the operators of
\mathcal{B}_{2}
by
i\mapsto i+r
so that
i\in\{r+1,\dots,r_{1}+r_{2}\}
. Define the
outer tensor product

\mathcal{B}_{1}\boxtimes\mathcal{B}_{2}
as the Cartesian product of
\mathcal{B}_{1}
and
\mathcal{B}_{2}
as a set, with ordered pairs denoted
x_{1}\boxtimes x_{2}
. We equip
\mathcal{B}_{1}\boxtimes\mathcal{B}_{2}
with the structure of a
\g_{1}\oplus\g_{2}
-crystal. For
i\in[r_{1}+r_{2}]
, the maps behave as follows,

\displaystyle\wt(x_{1}\boxtimes x_{2})

\displaystyle=\wt^{1}(x_{1})+\wt^{2}(x_{2}),

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}(x_{1}\boxtimes x_{2})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\varphi^{1}_{i}(x_{1})\quad\text{if }i\leq r_{1},\\
\varphi^{2}_{i}(x_{2})\quad\text{if }i>r_{1},\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{i}(x_{1}\boxtimes x_{2})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\varepsilon^{1}_{i}(x_{1})\quad\text{if }i\leq r_{1},\\
\varepsilon^{2}_{i}(x_{2})\quad\text{if }i>r_{1},\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle f_{i}(x_{1}\boxtimes x_{2})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}f^{1}_{i}(x_{1})\boxtimes x_{2}\quad\text{if }i\leq r_{1},\\
x_{1}\boxtimes f_{i}^{2}(x_{2})\quad\text{if }i>r_{1},\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle e_{i}(x_{1}\boxtimes x_{2})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}e^{1}_{i}(x_{1})\boxtimes x_{2}\quad\text{if }i\leq r_{1},\\
x_{1}\boxtimes e_{i}^{2}(x_{2})\quad\text{if }i>r_{1}.\\
\end{cases}

Again, we characterize the highest and lowest weight elements.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{1}
and
\mathcal{B}_{2}
be crystals as above.

(1)

x_{1}\boxtimes x_{2}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}\boxtimes\mathcal{B}_{2}
is a highest weight element if and only if
x_{1}
is a highest weight element in
\mathcal{B}_{1}
and
x_{2}
is a highest weight element in
\mathcal{B}_{2}
.

(2)

x_{1}\boxtimes x_{2}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}\boxtimes B_{2}
is a lowest weight element if and only if
x_{1}
is a lowest weight element in
\mathcal{B}_{1}
and
x_{2}
is a lowest weight element in
\mathcal{B}_{2}
.

Proof.

It is clear from the definitions that the
e_{i}
for
i\leq r
do not interact with the
e_{i}
for
i>r
. Similarly for
f_{i}
.
∎

3.3.
Normal crystals

Recall, we want to use crystals as a combinatorial model for finite dimensional representations of complex reductive Lie algebras. The category of seminormal crystals is too large to achieve this. The connection is made by restricting to normal crystals. There are a number of equivalent ways to define normal crystals and make the connection with representation theory: quantum groups
[
Kashiwara1995
]
, Littelmann paths
[
Littelmann1995
]
, and combinatorially
[
BumpSchilling2017
]
.

Once normal crystals are defined, we see that the category is semisimple in the sense that crystals break up into a disjoint union of connected subcrystals. The connected subcrystals correspond exactly to finite dimensional irreducible representations of the associated complex reductive Lie algebra and the disjoint union of connected crystals corresponds to the direct sum of irreducible representations. Levi branching and tensor products of crystals result in the same multiplicities as for finite dimensional representations of the associated Lie algebra.

The character of a crystal is defined as
\sum_{x\in\mathcal{B}}t^{\wt(x)}
and for normal crystals matches the character of the associated finite dimensional Lie algebra representation. In particular, a connected normal crystal has a unique highest weight and unique lowest weight. We will use below the fact that normal crystals are seminormal, so
\varepsilon_{i}
and
\varphi_{i}
can be used to detect highest and lowest weights.

3.4.
Crystal anti-isomorphisms

Let
\g
be a complex reductive Lie algebra with the notation above,
w_{0}
the long element of
W
and recall that there exists a permutation
i\mapsto i^{\prime}
of
[r]
such that

w_{0}(\alpha_{i})=-\alpha_{i^{\prime}}.

We say
S:\mathcal{C}_{1}\to\mathcal{C}_{2}
is a
crystal anti-isomorphism
if
S
is a bijection and

\wt(Sx)=w_{0}\wt(x)

e_{i^{\prime}}(Sx)=S(f_{i}(x)),

f_{i^{\prime}}(Sx)=S(e_{i}(x)),

\varepsilon_{i^{\prime}}(Sx)=\varphi_{i}(x)
,

\varphi_{i^{\prime}}(Sx)=\varepsilon_{i}(x)
,

and the same holds for
S^{-1}
. This is a slight generalization of the familiar definition of a crystal involution. A
crystal involution
is a crystal anti-isomorphism
S:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
, i.e. the domain and the range are the same set. Crystal involutions are also known as Lusztig involutions or Schützenberger involutions. Notice a crystal anti-isomorphism sends
e_{i}
to
f_{i^{\prime}}
,
f_{i}
to
e_{i^{\prime}}
, and exchanges highest and lowest weights.

Theorem 3.6
.

A connected normal crystal has a unique crystal involution.

Proof.

See
[
BumpSchilling2017
, Exercises 5.1, 5.2]
also
[
Lusztig1993
, Proposition 21.1.2]
.
∎

Crystal anti-isomorphisms are the connection between the branching rules we use here and the rules developed in
[
Kwon2018
,
KwonJang2021
,
LecouveyLenart2020
]
. To this end, we need to understand how crystal anti-isomorphisms should interact with tensor product.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{1}
and
\mathcal{B}_{2}
be normal crystals of
\g
and
S_{1}:\mathcal{B}_{1}\to\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime}
,
S_{2}:\mathcal{B}_{2}\to\mathcal{B}_{2}^{\prime}
be crystal anti-isomorphisms. Define the map

S:\mathcal{B}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{2}\to\mathcal{B}_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime}

by
S(x_{1}\otimes x_{2})=S_{2}(x_{2})\otimes S_{1}(x_{1})
. Then,
S
is a crystal anti-isomorphism.

Proof.

We just check that
S
satisfies the definition of crystal anti-isomorphism. First,
\wt(S(x_{1}\otimes x_{2}))=w_{0}(\wt(x_{1}\otimes x_{2}))
:

\displaystyle\wt(S(x_{1}\otimes x_{2}))

\displaystyle=\wt(S_{2}(x_{2})\otimes S_{1}(x_{1}))

\displaystyle=\wt(S_{2}(x_{2}))+\wt(S_{1}(x_{1}))

\displaystyle=w_{0}\wt(x_{2})+w_{0}\wt(x_{1})

\displaystyle=w_{0}(\wt(x_{1}\otimes x_{2})).

The first line is the definition of
S
. The second line is from the tensor product rule. The third line is from properties of the crystal anti-isomorphisms
S_{1},S_{2}
. The fourth line is from the linearity of
w_{0}
and the tensor product rule. Next,
e_{i^{\prime}}(S(x_{1}\otimes x_{2}))=S(f_{i}(x_{1}\otimes x_{2}))
:

\displaystyle e_{i^{\prime}}(S(x_{1}\otimes x_{2}))

\displaystyle=e_{i^{\prime}}(S_{2}(x_{2})\otimes S_{1}(x_{1}))

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}e_{i^{\prime}}(S_{2}(x_{2}))\otimes S_{1}(x_{1})\quad\text{if }\varepsilon_{i^{\prime}}(S_{2}(x_{2}))>\varphi_{i^{\prime}}(S_{1}(x_{1})),\\
S_{2}(x_{2})\otimes e_{i^{\prime}}(S_{1}(x_{1}))\quad\text{if }\varepsilon_{i^{\prime}}(S_{2}(x_{2}))\leq\varphi_{i^{\prime}}(S_{1}(x_{1})),\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}S_{2}(f_{i}(x_{2}))\otimes S_{1}(x_{1})\quad\text{if }\varphi_{i}(x_{2})>\varepsilon_{i}(x_{1}),\\
S_{2}(x_{2})\otimes S_{1}(f_{i}(x_{1}))\quad\text{if }\varphi_{i}(x_{2})\leq\varepsilon_{i}(x_{1}),\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}S(x_{1}\otimes f_{i}(x_{2}))\quad\text{if }\varepsilon_{i}(x_{1})<\varphi_{i}(x_{2}),\\
S(f_{i}(x_{1})\otimes x_{2})\quad\text{if }\varepsilon_{i}(x_{1})\geq\varphi_{i}(x_{2}),\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle=S(f_{i}(x_{1}\otimes x_{2})).

The first line is the definition of
S
. The second line is the tensor product rule. The third line comes from properties of the crystal anti-isomorphisms
S_{1},S_{2}
. The fourth is the definition of
S
. The fifth line is the tensor product rule. The remain verifications are similar and left to the reader.
∎

Next, we extend the definition of
S
to
k
-fold tensor products.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2},\dots,\mathcal{B}_{k}
be normal crystals of
\g
and
S_{1},\dots,S_{k}
be crystal anti-isomorphisms, where
S_{i}:\mathcal{B}_{i}\to\mathcal{B}_{i}^{\prime}
. Define the map

S:\mathcal{B}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{B}_{k}\to\mathcal{B}_{k}^{\prime}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{B}_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime}

by
S(x_{1}\otimes x_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k})=S_{k}(x_{k})\otimes\cdots\otimes S_{2}(x_{2})\otimes S_{1}(x_{1})
.

Theorem 3.9
.

S
is a crystal anti-isomorphism.

Proof.

Lemma
3.7
is the base case in an induction. Suppose the construction of
S
yields a crystal anti-isomorphism for a
(k-1)
-fold tensor product and consider
S:\mathcal{B}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{B}_{k}\to\mathcal{B}_{k}^{\prime}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{B}_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime}
. Let
S^{\prime}:\mathcal{B}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{B}_{k-1}\to\mathcal{B}_{k-1}^{\prime}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{B}_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime}
and notice

S((x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k-1})\otimes x_{k})=S_{k}(x_{k})\otimes S^{\prime}(x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k-1}).

S^{\prime}
is an anti-isomorphism by induction so
S
is an anti-isomorphism by Lemma
3.7
.
∎

4.
\GL_{n}
-crystals and tableaux

From here on the only crystals encountered will be normal
\gl_{n}
-crystals. In fact, we will only use
\GL_{n}
-crystals, i.e. normal
\gl_{n}
-crystals associated to representations of
\gl_{n}
that integrate to representations of
\GL_{n}
. Recall from Section
2.2
that this places constraints on the action of the center
\mathfrak{z}(\gl_{n})=\C
.

Normal
\gl_{n}
-crystals can be characterized axiomatically as in
[
Stembridge2003
]
. Everything we do in this paper can be understood at the level of characters. As the character of a
\GL_{n}
-crystal is equal to the character of the associated
\GL_{n}
representation, we move between the two notions freely below.

We focus on crystals associated to polynomial representation until the last two subsection. The last two subsections review a few facts about crystals of rational
\GL_{n}
representations. Rational
\GL_{n}
-crystals are only used for our treatment of graded multiplicities for
(\GL_{n}^{2},\GL_{n})
in Section
6
. They are not used elsewhere. In Section
4.1
, we review some combinatorial constructions with tableaux. In Section
4.2
we discuss crystals related to tensor products of the defining representation of
\GL_{n}
and the signature rule. In Section
4.3
we recall how to define a
\GL_{n}
-crystal structure on semistandard tableaux. In Section
4.4
we discuss plactic equivalence. In Section
4.5
we introduce two
\GL_{n}
-crystal anti-isomorphisms. In Section
4.6
we present some results related to the Littlewood-Richardson rule. Finally, in Sections
4.7
and
4.8
we review the facts we need about rational
\GL_{n}
-crystals.

4.1.
Tableaux and Knuth equivalence

As discussed in Section
2.3
,
\SST^{\lambda}_{n}
parametrizes a weight basis of the polynomial
\GL_{n}
irreducible representation with highest weight
\lambda
, so before discussing
\GL_{n}
-crystals, we briefly review some of the classical combinatorial theory of tableaux as presented in
[
Fulton1996
]
. In particular, we recall how to define a product on the set of semistandard tableaux. The combinatorial approach is through Knuth equivalence which is the equivalence relation on the set of words in the alphabet
\Z_{>0}
generated by elementary Knuth transformations. A
word

w
is just an ordered list of positive integers. We refer to
[
Fulton1996
, Chapter 2]
for the definitions. If
w
is Knuth equivalent to
w^{\prime}
, we will write
w\equiv_{K}w^{\prime}
.

For a skew-tableau
T
with shape
\lambda/\mu
we define the
(row) word
of
T
,
w(T)
to be the entries of
T
read left to right, bottom to top.

Example 4.1
.

Consider
\lambda=(5,4,4,2)
and
\mu=(3,1)
. Let

T=\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$4$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}.

Then
w(T)=44\cdot 2344\cdot 133\cdot 25
. We included the dots to make clear which row the entries are coming from. They are not part of the word.

There are two operations on tableaux we will need:
row bumping
and
sliding
. See
[
Fulton1996
, Chapter 1]
for definitions. These two operations lead to different ways of defining the product of tableaux. The tableau obtained from
T
by row bumping element
x
is denoted
T\leftarrow x
.

Given a skew-tableau, we can perform a sequence of slides to produce a semistandard tableau. The tableau so construction is called the
rectification
of
T
,
\Rect(T)
, and is independent of any choices. The key facts are

Proposition 4.2
.

(1)

w(T\leftarrow x)\equiv_{K}w(T)\cdot x
, here dot is just concatenation of words.

(2)

If one skew-tableau can be obtained from another by a sequence of slides, their row words are Knuth equivalent. In particular
w(\Rect(T))\equiv_{K}w(T).

Proof.

All facts can be found in
[
Fulton1996
]
: (1) is Proposition 2.1, (2) is Proposition 2.2.
∎

Theorem 4.3
.

[
Fulton1996
, Theorem 2.1]

Every word
x
is Knuth equivalent to the word of a unique tableau
P(x)
.

P(x)
can be constructed from
x=x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{k}
by repeatedly performing row-insertion,
P(x)=((\varnothing\leftarrow x_{1})\leftarrow x_{2})\leftarrow\cdots x_{k}
. Next, we recall three ways to define the product of two tableaux
T_{1},T_{2}
.

(1)

P(w(T_{1})\cdot w(T_{2}))
is constructed by concatenating the words of
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
then forming the unique tableau with word Knuth equivalent to
w(T_{1})\cdot w(T_{2})
.

(2)

T_{1}\leftarrow T_{2}
is constructed by row inserting
w(T_{2})=x_{1}x_{2}\dots x_{k}
into
T_{1}
, that is
T_{1}\leftarrow T_{2}=((T_{1}\leftarrow x_{1})\leftarrow x_{2})\leftarrow\cdots\leftarrow x_{k}
.

(3)

Let
T_{1}*T_{2}
be the skew-tableau with an
m\times n
rectangle of empty squares. Here
m
is the number of rows of
T_{2}
and
n
is the number of columns of
T_{1}
.
T_{1}
placed below the rectangle and
T_{2}
placed to the right of it. The third product construction is
\Rect(T_{1}*T_{2})
.

Theorem 4.4
.

[
Fulton1996
, Corollary 2.1.2]

The three product constructions above yield the same tableau.

Denote this unique tableau
T_{1}\cdot T_{2}
and call it the
product
of
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
. Hence, the set of all words in the alphabet
\Z_{>0}
modulo Knuth equivalence becomes a monoid, called the
plactic monoid
. Each element of the monoid is represented by a unique tableau and we can understand the multiplication by any of the three constructions above.

In a few instances it will be useful to extend the definition of product to skew-tableaux. We can do this by (1) or (3). The result is a semistandard tableau, in particular
T\cdot\varnothing=\Rect(T)
.

Lemma 4.5
.

For any skew-tableaux
T_{1},T_{2}
,

w(T_{1}\cdot T_{2})\equiv_{K}w(T_{1})\cdot w(T_{2}).

Proof.

By definition,
T_{1}\cdot T_{2}=P(w(T_{1})\cdot w(T_{2}))
and
w\big(P\big(w(T_{1})\cdot w(T_{2})\big)\big)\equiv_{K}w(T_{1})\cdot w(T_{2})
by Theorem
4.3
.
∎

This ends what we need about Knuth equivalence. We want to show that the product defined above in terms of Knuth equivalence is compatible with the tensor product of crystals. To do this, we recall how
\SST_{n}^{\lambda}
is given a crystal structure.

4.2.
\mathbb{B}_{n}^{\otimes k}
and the signature rule

Let
\mathbb{B}_{n}
denote the crystal associated to the defining representation of
\GL_{n}
.

Example 4.6
.

The crystal graph of
\mathbb{B}_{5}
is,

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{150.98463pt}{3.733pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

\wt(\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$i$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt})=\epsilon_{i}
and
e_{i},f_{i},\varepsilon_{i},\varphi_{i}
can be read from the graph by the seminormality of
\mathbb{B}_{5}
. For example,
\varphi_{i}(\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$j$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt})=\delta_{ij}
, the Kronecker delta function. Recall, an arrow labeled
i
indicates the action of
f_{i}
.

\mathbb{B}_{n}
is a normal crystal, so it behaves well under tensor product. Hence
\mathbb{B}_{n}^{\otimes k}
has the same structure as
(\C^{n})^{\otimes k}
and is well understood by Schur-Weyl duality,

Proposition 4.7

(Schur-Weyl Duality)
.

(\C^{n})^{\otimes k}=\bigoplus_{|\lambda|=k,\ell(\lambda)\leq n}\pi^{\lambda}_{S_{k}}\otimes\pi^{\lambda}_{\GL_{n}}.

It follows that any irreducible polynomial
\GL_{n}
representation can be found in some
(\C^{n})^{\otimes k}
and the multiplicity of
\pi^{\lambda}_{\GL_{n}}
is given by
\dim\pi^{\lambda}_{S_{k}}
. In terms of crystals, this implies we can find a model for the irreducible
\GL_{n}
-crystal of highest weight
\lambda
in
\mathbb{B}_{n}^{\otimes k}
where
k=|\lambda|
. In the next subsection, we will recall how to put a crystal structure on
\SST_{n}^{\lambda}
by embedding into
\mathbb{B}_{n}^{\otimes k}
and pulling back the structure from
\mathbb{B}_{n}^{\otimes k}
. Before that, we review a few facts about computing in
\mathbb{B}^{\otimes k}_{n}
, since all computations for connected polynomial
\GL_{n}
-crystals can be reduced to computation in
\mathbb{B}_{n}^{\otimes k}
via a choice of embedding.

The weight of the element
x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k}
of
\mathbb{B}^{\otimes k}_{n}
is just
\wt(x_{1})+\cdots+\wt(x_{k})
by the tensor product rule. There is a simple combinatorial algorithm for computing the action of
e_{i}
and
f_{i}
on an element
x_{1}\otimes x_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k}\in\mathbb{B}^{\otimes k}_{n}
known as the
signature rule
, see
[
BumpSchilling2017
, Section 2.4]
. It follows quickly from the tensor product rule and the structure of
\mathbb{B}_{n}
. The processes is as follows: write
-
above every
i
and
+
over every
i+1
. Successively cancel adjacent
(+-)
pairs until any remaining
-
’s occur before the first
+
, i.e. after the cancellations, the remaining
+
’s and
-
’s are in the form
-\cdots-+\cdots+
, with blanks interspersed. Suppose there are
a
remaining
+
’s and
b
remaining
-
’s. Then
e_{i}
acts on the term associated to the leftmost remaining
+
,
\varepsilon_{i}(x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k})=a
,
f_{i}
acts on the term associated to the rightmost remaining
-
, and
\varphi_{i}(x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k})=b
.

Example 4.8
.

Consider the following element of
\mathbb{B}^{\otimes 9}_{5}
,

x=\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$2$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$5$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont &\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$1$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule
