to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$2$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$\otimes$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont &\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$3$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$\otimes$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont &\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$3$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 1035.4pt}.

The weight of this element is
\wt(x)=\epsilon_{1}+3\epsilon_{2}+4\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon_{5}
. Suppose we want to compute
e_{2},\varepsilon_{2},f_{2},\varphi_{2}
for
x
. We start by putting
-
over every
2
and
+
over every
3
,

-

+

-

+

-

+

+

2

\otimes

3

Then we cancel all
(+-)
pairs,

-

+

+

2

to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$3$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 1035.4pt}.

We identify an element
x=x_{1}\otimes x_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k}
with the word
x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{k}
and write
x
for both. We extend Knuth equivalence to
\mathbb{B}^{\otimes k}_{n}
via this identification.

A word
x_{1}x_{2}\dots x_{k}
is a
Yamanouchi word
if every final segment
\{x_{j},\dots,x_{k}\}
contains at least as many
i
’s as it does
i+1
’s. A word
x_{1}\dots x_{k}
is an
anti-Yamanouchi word
if every initial segment
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{j}\}
contains at least as many
i+1
’s as it does
i
’s (up to
i=n
).

Proposition 4.9
.

(1)

An element of
\mathbb{B}_{n}^{\otimes k}
is a highest weight element if and only if it is a Yamanouchi word.

(2)

An element of
\mathbb{B}_{n}^{\otimes k}
is a lowest weight element if and only if it is an anti-Yamanouchi word.

Proof.

(1) is
[
BumpSchilling2017
, Proposition 8.2]
.

(2) Since
\mathbb{B}^{\otimes k}_{n}
is a seminormal crystal,
f_{i}(x_{1}\otimes x_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k})=0
if and only if
\varphi_{i}(x_{1}\otimes x_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k})=0
. By Lemma
3.4
,

\varphi_{i}(x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k})=\max_{j=1}^{k}\bigg(\sum_{h=1}^{j}\varphi_{i}(x_{h})-\sum_{h=1}^{j-1}\varepsilon_{i}(x_{h})\bigg).

Here
\varphi_{i}(x_{h})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }x_{h}=i,\\
0&\text{else.}\end{cases}
and
\varepsilon_{i}(x_{h})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }x_{h}=i+1,\\
0&\text{else.}\end{cases}

From this, it is immediate that
\varphi_{i}(x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k})=0
if and only if the number of
i+1
’s are greater than or equal to the number of
i
’s in every initial segment. Requiring this for
\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n-1}
is exactly the anti-Yamanouchi word condition.

∎

4.3.
\GL_{n}
-crystals of tableaux

Now we recall how to put a
\GL_{n}
-crystal structure on
\SST_{n}^{\lambda}
. More generally, we put a
\GL_{n}
-crystal structure on
\SST^{\lambda/\mu}_{n}
for any skew-diagram
\lambda/\mu
. To this end, we embed
\SST^{\lambda/\mu}_{n}
into
\mathbb{B}_{n}^{\otimes k}
where
k=|\lambda|-|\mu|
. We do this by row reading, but there are potentially many choices. Define
\RR:\SST^{\lambda/\mu}_{n}\to\mathbb{B}_{n}^{\otimes k}
by setting
\RR(T)
to be
x_{1}\otimes x_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k}
where
x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{k}=w(T)
, i.e. we just tensor the elements of the word of
T
together in order. We call
\RR(T)
the
row reading
of
T
.

Theorem 4.10
.

[
BumpSchilling2017
, Theorem 8.8]

The subset
\RR(\SST^{\lambda/\mu}_{n})
is a subcrystal of
\mathbb{B}_{n}^{\otimes k}
.

We give the set
\SST^{\lambda/\mu}_{n}
the crystal structure it inherits from
\mathbb{B}_{n}^{\otimes k}
via the embedding
\RR
. When
\SST^{\lambda/\mu}_{n}
is equipped with this crystal structure, we will denote it
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda/\mu}
. The weight of an element
T\in\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda/\mu}
is computed easily by the tensor product rule. Explicitly
\wt(T)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\epsilon_{i}
, where
a_{i}
is the number of
i
’s in the filling of
T
. This is the same definition of weight we used for tableaux in Section
2.3
.

We can compute
e_{i},f_{i},\varepsilon_{i}
, and
\varphi_{i}
by the signature rule on
\mathbb{B}^{\otimes k}_{n}
, e.g.
\varphi_{i}(T)=\varphi_{i}(\RR(T))
and
\varphi_{i}(\RR(T))
is given by the signature rule.

There are two instances of
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda/\mu}
that are particularly important for us:
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda}
and
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda^{\pi}}
where
\lambda
is a partition and
\lambda^{\pi}
is the
180^{\circ}
rotation described in Section
2.3
. As we will see, these provide two equivalent combinatorial models for the
\GL_{n}
irreducible representation of highest weight
\lambda
.

Example 4.11
.

Let
\lambda=(4,2,1)
and consider the following element of
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}_{5}
,

T=\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$1$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont &\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$1$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule Next, we establish notation for the highest and lowest weight elements in
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}_{n}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{n}
. Let
H^{\lambda}_{n}
denote the tableau on
\lambda
where the box
b
is filled by
\#\{\text{boxes above and including }b\}
.
H^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{n}\in\SST^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{n}
is defined identically. These are
highest weight tableaux
. Similarly, the
lowest weight tableaux
are denote
L^{\lambda}_{n}
and
L^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{n}
. A box
b
of a lowest weight tableau is filled by
n-\#\{\text{boxes below }b\}
.

Example 4.13
.

For
\lambda=(4,2,1)
, we have

H^{\lambda}_{5}=\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$5$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont\cr}}\kern 1728.73332pt}

We see that highest weights are easy to write in
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}_{n}
since
\lambda
is exactly the highest weight. Similarly, lowest weights are east to write in
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{n}
. Next, we justify our definitions in the case of
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}_{n}
. The analogous fact for
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{n}
is in Theorem
4.22
below.

Theorem 4.14
.

\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda}
is a connected crystal with unique highest weight element
H_{n}^{\lambda}
and lowest weight element
L_{n}^{\lambda}
.

Proof.

The connected assertion and highest weight assertion are contained in
[
BumpSchilling2017
, Theorem 3.2]
. The definition of
L^{\lambda}_{n}
ensures
\RR(L^{\lambda}_{n})
is an anti-Yamanouchi word: if box
b
in
L^{\lambda}_{n}
is filled by
n-i
with
i>0
, then there are
i
boxes below
b
. In particular, the box directly below
b
is filled with
n-i+1
. This comes before
b
in the row reading. It follows that
\#\{n-i+1\}\geq\#\{n-i\}
for all
i\in[n-1]
and for all initial segments of
\RR(L^{\lambda}_{n})
.
∎

4.4.
Plactic equivalence

There are two equivalence relations that are important to us: Knuth equivalence
\equiv_{K}
discussed above and plactic equivalence denoted
\equiv
. Plactic equivalence comes from isomorphisms in the monoidal category of normal
\GL_{n}
-crystals.

Definition 4.15
.

[
BumpSchilling2017
, Definition 8.1]

Let
\mathcal{B}_{1}
and
\mathcal{B}_{2}
be normal
\GL_{n}
-crystals and
x_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{i}
. Let
\mathcal{B}_{i}^{\prime}
be the connected component of
\mathcal{B}_{i}
that contains
x_{i}
. If
\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{B}_{2}^{\prime}
and if the unique isomorphism
\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime}\to B_{2}^{\prime}
takes
x_{1}
to
x_{2}
, then we say
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
are
plactically equivalent
, and write
x_{1}\equiv x_{2}
.

Example 4.16
.

For any
T\in\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda/\mu}
,
T\equiv\RR(T)
by construction of the crystal structure on
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda/\mu}_{n}
.

Example 4.17
.

The tableau
T
from Example
4.11
is plactically equivalent to the tableau
T
from Example
4.12
via the isomorphism of Theorem
4.22
(2) below.

Theorem 4.18
.

x,y\in\mathbb{B}_{n}^{\otimes k}
are plactically equivalent if and only if they are Knuth equivalent.

Proof.

Knuth equivalence implies plactic equivalence is
[
BumpSchilling2017
, Theorem 8.4]
.

Plactic equivalence implies Knuth equivalence can be deduced as follows. By
[
BumpSchilling2017
, Theorem 8.6 (i)]
,
x\equiv P(x)
. From this, we see
P(x)\equiv P(y)
if and only if
x\equiv y
. Notice
P(x)\equiv P(y)
if and only if
P(x)=P(y)
: say
P(x)\in\mathcal{B}^{\mu}_{n}
and
P(y)\in\mathcal{B}^{\nu}_{n}
then by definition of plactic equivalence, we must have
\mu=\nu
and there is only one automorphism of
\mathcal{B}^{\mu}_{n}
, the identity map, so
P(x)=P(y)
. Now Theorem
4.3
gives
x\equiv_{K}P(x)
. Since
P(x)=P(y)
, we have
x\equiv_{K}P(x)=P(y)\equiv_{K}y
.
∎

Hence, plactic equivalence and Knuth equivalence both yield the same monoid on the set of all words in the alphabet
[n]
.

Proposition 4.19
.

Let
T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda_{1}/\mu_{1}},\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda_{2}/\mu_{2}}
. The following are plactically equivalent.

(1)

T_{1}\otimes T_{2}
in
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda_{1}/\mu_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda_{2}/\mu_{2}}
,

(2)

\RR(T_{1})\otimes\RR(T_{2})
in
\mathbb{B}_{n}^{\otimes(|\lambda_{1}/\mu_{1}|+|\lambda_{2}/\mu_{2}|)}
,

(3)

T_{1}\cdot T_{2}
in
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
, where
\nu
is the shape of
T_{1}\cdot T_{2}
.

Proof.

The equivalence between (1) and (2) comes from observing
T_{i}\equiv\RR(T_{i})
(see Example
4.16
) and that
T_{1}\equiv\RR(T_{1})
and
T_{2}\equiv\RR(T_{2})
implies
T_{1}\otimes T_{2}\equiv\RR(T_{1})\otimes\RR(T_{2})
, see
[
BumpSchilling2017
, pg. 113]
.

Next we show (2) and (3) are equivalent. By Lemma
4.5
,
\RR(T_{1}\cdot T_{2})\equiv_{K}\RR(T_{1})\cdot\RR(T_{2})
so by Theorem
4.18
,
\RR(T_{1}\cdot T_{2})\equiv\RR(T_{1})\otimes\RR(T_{2})
. Since
\RR(T_{1}\cdot T_{2})\equiv T_{1}\cdot T_{2}
, the result follows.
∎

Proposition 4.20
.

\Rect(T)\equiv T
for any skew-tableau
T
.

Proof.

\RR(\Rect(T))\equiv_{K}\RR(T)
by Proposition
4.2
so by Theorem
4.18
,
\RR(\Rect(T))\equiv\RR(T)
. Hence by Example
4.16
,
\Rect(T)\equiv T
.
∎

4.5.
\GL_{n}
-crystal anti-isomorphisms

There are two
\GL_{n}
-crystal anti-isomorphisms we will use. First, Berenstein and Zelevinsky showed that the unique crystal involution on a connected
\GL_{n}
-crystal of tableaux is given by
Schützenberger evacuation
, see
[
BerensteinZelevinsky1996
, Section 8]
. We recall, see
[
Lenart2007
, 6-7]
, that this map can be realized by the following procedure:

(1)

Rotate the tableau
180^{\circ}
.

(2)

Complement the entries via the permutation
i\mapsto w_{0}(i)=n+1-i
.

(3)

Rectify the skew-tableau.

Let
S:\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda}\to\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda}
be the map defined by applying the Schützenberger evacuation. We extend
S
to tensor products as in Definition
3.8
. This is the first crystal anti-isomorphism that we will use.

The second, comes from just applying the first two steps above. Denote this map
S^{\pi}
. Notice the map
S^{\pi}
makes sense for any crystal of skew-tableaux. We define it in this generality, so
S^{\pi}:\mathcal{B}^{\lambda/\mu}_{n}\to\mathcal{B}^{\lambda^{\pi}/\mu^{\pi}}_{n}
. Here
\lambda^{\pi}/\mu^{\pi}
is constructed identically to
\lambda^{\pi}
in Section
2.3
, namely rotating
\lambda/\mu
by
180^{\circ}
about the origin and translating by
n+1
. By construction
S:\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}_{n}\to\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}_{n}
is just
\Rect\circ S^{\pi}
.

To show that
S
and
S^{\pi}
are crystal anti-isomorphisms, we start with the unique involution
I:\mathbb{B}_{n}\to\mathbb{B}_{n}
which takes
i\mapsto w_{0}(i)=n+1-i
. We denote
w_{0}(i)
by
\widetilde{\iota}
. By the construction in Definition
3.8
, this leads to a crystal involution on
\mathbb{B}^{\otimes k}_{n}
, namely

\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$\mapsto$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$\otimes$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont &\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$\cdots$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont &\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$\otimes$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont &\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$\widetilde{\iota}_{2}$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$\otimes$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont &\widetilde{\iota}_1\cr}}\kern 2091.78891pt}.

Denote this involution
I^{\otimes k}:\mathbb{B}_{n}^{\otimes k}\to\mathbb{B}_{n}^{\otimes k}
.

Lemma 4.21
.

S^{\pi}:\mathcal{B}^{\lambda/\mu}_{n}\to\mathcal{B}^{\lambda^{\pi}/\mu^{\pi}}_{n}
is a crystal anti-isomorphism.

Proof.

Since the crystal structures on
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda/\mu}_{n}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda^{\pi}/\mu^{\pi}}_{n}
are inherited from
\RR(\mathcal{B}^{\lambda/\mu}_{n})
and
\RR(\mathcal{B}^{\lambda^{\pi}/\mu^{\pi}}_{n})
, it is sufficient to show
S^{\pi}
is a crystal anti-isomorphism on the level of row words. Notice step (1) reverses row words and step (2) applies
w_{0}
. Hence, we see at the level of row words,
S^{\pi}
is exactly
I^{\otimes k}
restricted to the row words of
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda/\mu}_{n}
. Since
S^{\pi}
is clearly bijective,
S^{\pi}
is a crystal anti-isomorphism.
∎

We extend
S^{\pi}
to tensor products as in Definition
3.8
.

Theorem 4.22
.

(1)

\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda^{\pi}}
is a connected crystal with unique highest weight element
H_{n}^{\lambda^{\pi}}
and lowest weight element
L_{n}^{\lambda^{\pi}}
.

(2)

\Rect:\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda^{\pi}}\to\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda}
taking
T
to
\Rect(T)
is a crystal isomorphism.

(3)

\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda/\mu}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda^{\pi}/\mu^{\pi}}
.

(4)

S:\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}_{n}\to\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}_{n}
is a crystal involution.

Proof.

(1) Consider
S^{\pi}:\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}_{n}\to\mathcal{B}^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{n}
. This is a bijection taking highest weights to lowest weights. Since
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}_{n}
has a unique highest weight
\lambda
,
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{n}
has a unique lowest weight
w_{0}(\lambda)=\lambda^{\pi}
. It follows from normality of
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{n}
and the structure of
\gl_{n}
irreducible representations that
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{n}
has a unique highest weight
\lambda
. It is easy to see
S^{\pi}(H^{\lambda}_{n})=L^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{n}
and
S^{\pi}(L^{\lambda}_{n})=H^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{n}
.

(2) By Proposition
4.20
,
\Rect(T)\equiv T
. Hence, it suffices to show
\Rect
is bijective from
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{n}\to\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}_{n}
. Since
|\mathcal{B}^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{n}|=|\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}_{n}|
it suffices to show
\Rect
is injective. Let
T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathcal{B}^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{n}
with
T_{1}\neq T_{2}
. Then
T_{1}\not\equiv T_{2}
since these are distinct elements in a connected normal crystal and the only crystal automorphism is the identity. Hence, by Proposition
4.20
,
\Rect(T_{1})\equiv T_{1}\not\equiv T_{2}\equiv\Rect(T_{2})
, so
\Rect(T_{1})\neq\Rect(T_{2})
.

(3)
S^{\pi}
gives a bijection between highest weights of
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda/\mu}_{n}
and lowest weights of
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda^{\pi}/\mu^{\pi}}_{n}
, the lowest weights of
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda^{\pi}/\mu^{\pi}}_{n}
being the same as the lowest weights of
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda/\mu}_{n}
. Since normal crystals are uniquely determined by lowest weights,
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda/\mu}_{n}\cong\mathcal{B}^{\lambda^{\pi}/\mu^{\pi}}_{n}
.

(4)
S=\Rect\circ S^{\pi}
. By Lemma
4.21
,
S^{\pi}
is a crystal anti-isomorphism.
\Rect
is a crystal isomorphism by (2). The composition of a crystal isomorphism and crystal anti-isomorphism is clearly a crystal anti-isomorphism. In this case, an involution.
∎

. Rectifying, we get

and rectifying again we arrive back at

.

4.6.
The Littlewood-Richardson rule

The key to our approach to the Littlewood-Richardson rule is the following seesaw pair, see
[
BumpSchilling2017
, Chapter 9]
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\GL_{n}^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\GL_{n}^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\GL_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\GL_{2n},}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\supset}

where on the left
\GL_{n}
is included diagonally in
\GL_{n}^{2}
and on the right
\GL_{n}^{2}
is included as the Levi subgroup of a parabolic in
\GL_{2n}
. All groups act on the symmetric algebra on
2n\times n
matrices,
S(M_{2n,n}(\C))
, and each row of the diagram above is a dual pair, see
[
BumpSchilling2017
, Appendix 2]
for details. The usual seesaw argument allows us to conclude,

Proposition 4.24
.

[
BumpSchilling2017
, Theorem 9.4]

\mult(\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}_{n},\mathcal{B}^{\mu}_{n}\boxtimes\mathcal{B}^{\nu}_{n})=\mult(\mathcal{B}^{\mu}_{n}\boxtimes\mathcal{B}^{\nu}_{n},\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}_{2n}).

We can find the non-Levi branching multiplicity of
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}_{n}
in
\Res^{\GL^{2}_{n}}_{\GL_{n}}(\mathcal{B}^{\mu}_{n}\boxtimes\mathcal{B}^{\nu}_{n})=\mathcal{B}^{\mu}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\nu}_{n}
coming from the left side by counting highest weight elements in
\mathcal{B}^{\mu}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\nu}_{n}
with weight
\lambda
. Alternatively, we could count lowest weight elements. In either case, the underlying tool is the tensor product rule. Notice by Proposition
3.3
the highest weight elements in
\mathcal{B}^{\mu}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\nu}_{n}
are of the form
T\otimes H_{n}^{\nu}
for some
T\in\mathcal{B}^{\mu}_{n}
. Hence we can parametrize highest weights in terms of elements of
\mathcal{B}^{\mu}_{n}
. Similarly, lowest weights are of the form
L_{n}^{\mu}\otimes T
for some
T\in\mathcal{B}^{\nu}_{n}
and can be parameterized in terms of elements of
\mathcal{B}^{\nu}_{n}
. We make the following definitions:

\displaystyle\HC_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\GL_{n})

\displaystyle\coloneq\{T\in\mathcal{B}^{\mu}_{n}:T\otimes H_{n}^{\nu}\equiv H_{n}^{\lambda}\},

\displaystyle\LC_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\GL_{n})

\displaystyle\coloneq\{T\in\mathcal{B}^{\nu}_{n}:L_{n}^{\mu}\otimes T\equiv L_{n}^{\lambda}\}.

\HC_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\GL_{n})
are called
(highest weight) companion tableaux
and
\LC_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\GL_{n})
are called
lowest weight companion tableaux
. By Theorem
4.22
,
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\delta}\cong\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\delta^{\pi}}
so we can replace any Young diagram
\delta
in this definition with
\delta^{\pi}
. For example
\HC^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{\mu\nu^{\pi}}(\GL_{n})
has the same cardinality as
\HC_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\GL_{n})
.

The Levi branching coming from the right side of the seesaw leads to the standard Littlewood-Richardson tableaux. Again, we can determine these multiplicities by counting highest or lowest weight elements of
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\mu}\boxtimes\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\nu}
in the
\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n}
crystal obtained by branching
\mathcal{B}_{2n}^{\lambda}
.

Theorem 4.25
.

[
BumpSchilling2017
, Theorem 8.14]

Let
\lambda
be a partition of length
\leq n
.

\Res^{\GL_{2n}}_{\GL_{n}^{2}}(\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}_{2n})=\bigoplus_{\mu\subseteq\lambda}(\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\mu}\boxtimes\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda/\mu}).

By Proposition
3.5
, to find the multiplicity of
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\mu}\boxtimes\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\nu}
in
\Res^{\GL_{2n}}_{\GL_{n}^{2}}(\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}_{2n})
we just need to count the highest or lowest weights of
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\nu}
in
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda/\mu}
. We make the following definitions:

\displaystyle\HL_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\GL_{n})

\displaystyle\coloneq\{T\in\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda/\mu}:T\equiv H_{n}^{\nu}\},

\displaystyle\LL_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\GL_{n})

\displaystyle\coloneq\{T\in\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda/\mu}:T\equiv L_{n}^{\nu}\}.

\HL_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\GL_{n})
are called
(highest weight) Littlewood-Richardson tableaux
and
\LL_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\GL_{n})
are called
lowest weight Littlewood-Richardson tableaux
. Again, there are many variations of this definition. By Theorem
4.22
,
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda/\mu}_{n}\cong\mathcal{B}^{\lambda^{\pi}/\mu^{\pi}}_{n}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\nu}_{n}\cong\mathcal{B}^{\nu^{\pi}}_{n}
so we can apply
\pi
to
\lambda/\mu
or
\nu
or both. An example that will be important later is
\HL^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{\mu^{\pi}\nu}
. From the above discussion, we conclude

Theorem 4.26
.

For
\lambda,\mu,\nu
all of ambient length
n
,

|\HC_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\GL_{n})|=|\LC_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\GL_{n})|=|\HL_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\GL_{n})|=|\LL_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\GL_{n})|.

\pi
can be placed on the
\lambda,\mu,\nu
in this theorem as described above. The common cardinality of these sets is the
Littlewood-Richardson coefficient
denoted
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}
and as we recall below, is independent of
n
.

Next, we provide characterizations of these highest (lowest) weight elements in the two cases we will use in applications. Similar statements can be made for the other cases. Recall, we use
\mu^{*}
to denote the diagram indexing the dual representation of
\mu
and
\mu_{0}
to denote the projection of
\mu
onto the semisimple weight lattice of
\sll_{n}
, see Theorem
2.1
and the surrounding discussion. In (2) below,
\mu_{0},\varphi(T)^{*}\in P_{++}(\sll_{n})
are elements of the semisimple weight lattice and the partial order
\geq
is the one defined on
P(\sll_{n})=P(\g^{\prime})
in Section
2.1
.

Proposition 4.27
.

The following sets are equal.

(1)

\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\GL_{n})
,

(2)

\{T\in\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\nu}:\mu_{0}\geq\varphi(T)^{*}\text{ and }\wt(L^{\mu}_{n})+\wt(T)=\wt(L^{\lambda}_{n})
,

(3)

\{T\in\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\nu}:\RR(L_{n}^{\mu})\otimes\RR(T)\text{ is an anti-Yamanouchi word and }\wt(L^{\mu}_{n})+\wt(T)=\wt(L^{\lambda}_{n})\}
,

(4)

\{T\in\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\nu}:L_{n}^{\mu}\cdot T\equiv L_{n}^{\lambda}\}
.

As above, we can place
\pi
on any combination of
\lambda,\mu,\nu
without impacting the cardinality of the set.

Proof.

We show (2), (3), and (4) are equivalent to (1).

(2) By Proposition
3.3
,
L^{\mu}_{n}\otimes T\equiv L_{n}^{\lambda}
if and only if
-\wt_{ss}(L^{\mu}_{n})\geq\varphi(T)
and
\wt(L^{\mu}_{n})+\wt(T)=\wt(L^{\lambda}_{n})
. By the definition of
L^{\mu}_{n}
and Lemma
2.2
,
-\wt_{ss}(L^{\mu}_{n})=-ss(w_{0}(\mu))=\mu_{0}^{*}\geq\varphi(T)
. Applying
-w_{0}
to both sides of the inequality, (2) follows.

(3) By Proposition
4.9
,
\RR(L^{\mu}_{n})\otimes\RR(T)
is an anti-Yamanouchi word if and only if
\RR(L^{\mu}_{n})\otimes\RR(T)
is a lowest weight element. By Example
4.16
,
\RR(L^{\mu}_{n})\otimes\RR(T)\equiv L^{\mu}_{n}\otimes T
. Since
\wt(L^{\mu}_{n})+\wt(T)=\wt(L^{\lambda}_{n})
it follows that
\RR(L^{\mu}_{n})\otimes\RR(T)
is an anti-Yamanouchi word if and only if
L^{\mu}_{n}\otimes T\equiv L_{n}^{\lambda}
.

(4)
L_{n}^{\lambda}\equiv L_{n}^{\mu}\otimes T\equiv L_{n}^{\mu}\cdot T
. The first equivalence is the definition of
\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\GL_{n})
. The second equivalence is Proposition
4.19
.
∎

Now for
\HL^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\GL_{n})
,

Proposition 4.28
.

The following sets are equal.

(1)

\HL^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\GL_{n})

(2)

\{T\in\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda/\mu}:\varepsilon(T)=0\text{ and }\wt(T)=\nu\}
.

(3)

\{T\in\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda/\mu}:\RR(T)\text{ is a Yamanouchi word and }\wt(T)=\nu\}
.

(4)

\{T\in\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda/\mu}:\Rect(T)=H_{n}^{\nu}\}
.

As above, we can place
\pi
on any combination of
\lambda/\mu
and
\nu
without impacting the cardinality of the set.

Proof.

We show (2), (3), and (4) are equivalent to (1).

(2) follows immediately from the definition of highest weight element and the fact that
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda/\mu}
is seminormal.

(3) By Proposition
4.9
,
\RR(T)
is a Yamanouchi word if and only if
\RR(T)
is a highest weight element. Since
\wt(T)=\nu
, this happens if and only if
\RR(T)\equiv H^{\nu}_{n}
. By Example
4.16
,
T\equiv\RR(T)\equiv H^{\nu}_{n}
.

(4) By Proposition
4.2
, rectification preserves Knuth equivalence so
\Rect(T)=H^{\nu}_{n}
if and only if
\RR(T)\equiv_{K}\RR(H^{\nu}_{n})
. By Theorem
4.18
,
\RR(T)\equiv\RR(H^{\nu}_{n})
and by Example
4.16
,
T\equiv H^{\nu}_{n}
.
∎

Corollary 4.29
.

For
n,m\geq\ell(\lambda)
,
|\HL^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\GL_{m})|=|\HL^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\GL_{n})|=c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}
, where
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}
is the standard Littlewood-Richardson coefficient.

Proof.

A skew-tableau
T
on shape
\lambda/\mu
such that
\RR(T)
is a Yamanouchi word can only involve numbers up to
\ell(\lambda)
, so the set in Proposition
4.28
(3) is the same for any
n,m\geq\ell(\lambda)
.
∎

Next, we define bijections between these sets in a few instances that will be important later. Similar arguments can provide many more bijections.

Definition 4.30
.

Let
\phi
be the map that takes
T\in\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\GL_{n})
to the skew-tableau
\phi(T)
on
\lambda^{\pi}/\nu^{\pi}
. The
i^{\text{th}}
row of
\phi(T)
is filled by the numbers of the rows of
T
containing entry
i
, i.e. an
i
in the
j^{\text{th}}
row of
T
corresponds to a
j
in the
i^{\text{th}}
row of
\phi(T)
. Row numbers are recorded with multiplicity.

See
[
vanLeeuwen2001
]
. We show in Theorem
4.31
that
\phi
is well-defined.

Theorem 4.31
.

\phi
is a bijection from
\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\GL_{n})
to
\HL^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{\mu^{\pi}\nu}(\GL_{n})
.
\phi^{-1}
is given by the same rule as
\phi
.

Proof.

T\in\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\GL_{n})
, so
T
has shape
\nu
and
\wt(T)=\wt(L^{\lambda}_{n})-\wt(L^{\mu}_{n})=\lambda^{\pi}/\mu^{\pi}
. Hence
\phi
is a well-defined map to
\lambda^{\pi}/\mu^{\pi}
with filling
\nu
.

Next, notice that the strictly increasing columns condition on
T
ensures that
\RR(\phi(T))
is a Yamanouchi word. Assume
i>1
and let
\{x_{i1},\dots,x_{il}\}
be the filling of row
i
in
T
. Then we record an
i
in rows
x_{i1},\dots,x_{il}
of
\phi(T)
. Since the columns of
T
are strictly increasing, there exists an
x_{i-1j}<x_{ij}
in row
i-1
above
x_{ij}
for
j=1,\dots l
. Thus, for any
i
in row
x_{ij}
of
\phi(T)
, we record an
i-1
in row
x_{i-1j}
. Since
x_{i-1j}<x_{ij}
, there are at least as many
i-1
as there are
i
in any final segment of
\RR(\phi(T))
. Hence
\RR(\phi(T))
is a Yamanouchi word.

To show
\phi(T)
is semistandard, it suffices to show the columns are strictly increasing. By our characterization of
\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\GL_{n})
in Proposition
4.27
, we see
\RR(L^{\mu}_{n})\otimes\RR(T)
is an anti-Yamanouchi word. Write this word as
w=m_{1}\cdots m_{j}x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{k}
, where
m_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes m_{j}=\RR(L^{\mu}_{n})
and
x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k}=\RR(T)
. The fact that
\RR(L^{\mu}_{n})\otimes\RR(T)
is anti-Yamanouchi implies that every initial segment
m_{1}\cdots m_{j}x_{1}\cdots x_{i}
containing the first
j+i
terms of
w
is plactically equivalent to a lowest weight tableau
L^{\lambda_{i}^{\pi}}_{n}
. So we get an increasing chain of lowest weight tableaux

L^{\mu^{\pi}}_{n}=L_{n}^{\lambda^{\pi}_{0}}\subset L^{\lambda^{\pi}_{1}}_{n}\subset L^{\lambda^{\pi}_{2}}_{n}\subset\cdots\subset L^{\lambda^{\pi}_{k}}_{n}=L^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{n},

where
\lambda_{i+1}^{\pi}/\lambda_{i}^{\pi}
contains one box
b
in row
x_{i+1}
.
\phi(T)
is constructed by putting the
T
row number of
x_{i+1}
, denoted
\text{row}(x_{i+1})
in
b
for
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
. There is no box above
b
, otherwise
\lambda^{\pi}_{i}
would not be a rotated Young diagram and if
x_{i+1}<n
, there is a box
c
below
b
. If
c\not\in\mu^{\pi}
, let
r
be the filling of
c
.
r
is the row number of some
x_{l}\in\{x_{1},\dots,x_{i}\}
. Since
c
is one row lower than
b
in
\lambda_{i+1}^{\pi}
,
x_{l}=x_{i+1}+1
and since
c\in\lambda_{i}
and
b\not\in\lambda_{i}
,
l<i+1
. Because the rows of
T
are weakly increasing, the fact that
l<i+1
and
x_{l}>x_{i+1}
, implies that
x_{l}
must be from a different row of
T
than
x_{i+1}
. Since rows of
T
are read bottom to top,
\text{row}(x_{l})>\text{row}(x_{i+1})
. Recalling that the filling of
b
is
\text{row}(x_{i+1})
and the filling of
c
is
\text{row}(x_{l})
, we see the columns of
\phi(T)
are strictly increasing. So
\phi
maps to
\HL^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{\mu^{\pi}\nu}(\GL_{n})
.

It is easy to see
\phi
is injective. Since
|\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\GL_{n})|=|\HL^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{\mu^{\pi}\nu}(\GL_{n})|
by Theorem
4.26
, we conclude
\phi
is a bijection. It is then clear that
\phi^{-1}
is given by the same rule.
∎

Example 4.32
.

Let
\lambda=(4,4,2,1)
,
\mu=(3,1)
, and
\nu=(4,2,1)
. The tableau

2

4

3

5

4

in
\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\GL_{5})
corresponds under
\phi
to

1

2

1

2

3

1

4

2

5

.

Theorem 4.33
.

S:\mathcal{B}^{\nu}_{n}\to\mathcal{B}^{\nu}_{n}
restricts to a bijection between
\HC_{\nu\mu}^{\lambda}(\GL_{n})
and
\LC_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\GL_{n})
and
S^{\pi}:\mathcal{B}^{\nu^{\pi}}_{n}\to\mathcal{B}^{\nu}_{n}
restricts to a bijection between
\HC_{\nu^{\pi}\mu}^{\lambda}(\GL_{n})
and
\LC_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\GL_{n})
.

Proof.

Let
T\in\HC^{\lambda}_{\nu\mu}(\GL_{5})
so
T\otimes H_{n}^{\mu}\equiv H_{n}^{\lambda}
. Applying
S
, we see
S(T\otimes H_{n}^{\mu})\equiv S(H_{n}^{\lambda})=L_{n}^{\lambda}
by Theorem
4.22
and the definition of crystal anti-isomorphism. Using the extension of
S
to tensor products, Definition
3.8
,
S(T\otimes H_{n}^{\mu})=S(H_{n}^{\mu})\otimes S(T)=L_{n}^{\mu}\otimes S(T)
. Hence
L_{n}^{\lambda}\equiv L_{n}^{\mu}\otimes S(T)
and
S(T)\in\LC_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\GL_{n})
. Similarly, if
T\in\LC_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\GL_{n})
, then
L_{n}^{\mu}\otimes T\equiv L_{n}^{\lambda}
. Applying
S
, we find
S(T)\otimes H_{n}^{\mu}\equiv H_{n}^{\lambda}
, so
S(T)\in\HC^{\lambda}_{\nu\mu}(\GL_{n})
.

The argument for
\HC^{\lambda}_{\nu^{\pi}\mu}(\GL_{n})
and
\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\GL_{n})
is the same, using
S^{\pi}
in place of
S
.
∎

4.7.
Rational
\GL_{n}
-crystals

Let
\mathbb{B}_{n}^{*}
denote the dual crystal of the defining representation of
\GL_{n}
.

.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{150.98463pt}{3.733pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

\wt(\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$\overline{i}$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt})=-\epsilon_{i}
and
e_{i},f_{i},\varepsilon_{i},\varphi_{i}
can be read from the graph.

Let
\lambda=(\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})\in\widehat{\GL}_{n}
. Similar to the case of polynomial
\GL_{n}
-crystals, we put a crystal structure on the rational
\GL_{n}
-tableaux
\mathcal{T}^{\lambda}_{\GL_{n}}
of Definition
2.5
by embedding into
(\mathbb{B}_{n}^{*})^{\otimes k_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{n}^{\otimes k_{2}}
where
k_{1}=|\lambda^{-}|
and
k_{2}=|\lambda^{+}|
. As discussed in
[
STEMBRIDGE1987
]
, we can think of the ordered pair of partitions
(\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})
as a staircase
(\lambda^{+}_{1},\lambda^{+}_{2},\dots,-\lambda^{-}_{2},\lambda^{-}_{1})\in\Z^{n}
which corresponds to the highest weight
\lambda_{1}^{+}\epsilon_{1}+\lambda_{2}^{+}\epsilon_{2}+\cdots-\lambda_{2}^{-}\epsilon_{n-1}-\lambda_{1}^{-}\epsilon_{n}
. Similarly, we can think of a rational tableau as a staircase tableau by rotating the tableau on
\lambda^{-}
by
180^{\circ}
and negating the entries. We embed the staircase tableaux into
(\mathbb{B}_{n}^{*})^{\otimes k_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{n}^{\otimes k_{2}}
by row reading.

Example 4.35
.

Here is an example of the correspondence between rational tableaux and staircase tableaux for
\GL_{5}
.

to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$5$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 1035.4pt}.

As in the case of polynomial
\GL_{n}
-crystals,
\RR(\mathcal{T}^{\lambda}_{\GL_{n}})
is a subcrystal of
(\mathbb{B}_{n}^{*})^{\otimes k_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{n}^{\otimes k_{2}}
. We give
\mathcal{T}^{\lambda}_{\GL_{n}}
the crystal structure it inherits via this embedding and denote the resulting crystal
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}_{n}
, see
[
Kwon2009
, Section 3.4]
.

We extend the signature rule to
(\mathbb{B}_{n}^{*})^{\otimes k_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{n}^{\otimes k_{2}}
as follows. To compute the action of
e_{i}
and
f_{i}
, write
-
above every
i
and
+
over every
i+1
as in the
\mathbb{B}_{n}
case. In addition, we write
-
above every
\overline{i+1}
and
+
over every
\overline{i}
. Then we proceed as before: successively cancel adjacent
(+-)
pairs until any remaining
-
’s occur before the first
+
. Suppose there are
a
remaining
+
’s and
b
remaining
-
’s. Then
e_{i}
acts on the term associated to the leftmost remaining
+
,
\varepsilon_{i}(x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k})=a
,
f_{i}
acts on the term associated to the rightmost remaining
-
, and
\varphi_{i}(x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k})=b
.

Example 4.36
.

Suppose we want to compute
e_{4},\varepsilon_{4},f_{4},\varphi_{4}
for the example above. We start by putting
-
over every
4
and every
\overline{5}
and
+
over every
5
and every
\overline{4}
.

-

+

-

+

-

+

\overline{5}

\otimes

5

Then cancel
(+-)
pairs,

-

+

\overline{5}

\otimes

5

From this we see
\varepsilon_{4}(\RR(T))=\varphi_{4}(\RR(T))=1
,
e_{4}
changes the
5
below the
+
to a
4
and
f_{4}
changes the
\overline{5}
below the
-
to a
\overline{4}
. This pulls back to the staircase tableau. For example,

We extend the definition of
H_{n}^{\lambda}
and
L_{n}^{\lambda}
to
\lambda=(\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})
. Continue to view
\lambda
as a staircase. Let
H^{\lambda}_{n}
denote the tableau on
\lambda
where all boxes
b^{+}\in\lambda^{+}
are filled by
\#\{\text{boxes above and including }b^{+}\}
and all boxes
b^{-}\in\lambda^{-}
are filled by
\overline{n-\#\{\text{boxes above }b^{-}\}}
. Let
L^{\lambda}_{n}
denote the tableau on
\lambda
were all boxes
b^{+}\in\lambda^{+}
are filled by
n-\#\{\text{boxes below }b^{+}\}
and all boxes
b^{-}\in\lambda^{-}
are filled by
\overline{n-\#\{\text{boxes below and including }b^{-}\}}
.

Example 4.37
.

Let
\lambda=(\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})
with
\lambda^{+}=(3,2,0,0,0)
and
\lambda^{-}=(3,1,0,0,0)
. The associated staircase is
(3,2,0,-1,-3)
and corresponding highest weight is
3\epsilon_{1}+2\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{4}-3\epsilon_{5}
. The long Weyl group element
w_{0}
takes the highest weight
\lambda
to the the lowest weight
w_{0}(\lambda)=3\epsilon_{5}+2\epsilon_{4}-\epsilon_{2}-3\epsilon_{1}
. We can see this reflected in
H^{\lambda}_{5}
and
L^{\lambda}_{5}
below.

Theorem 4.38
.

For
\lambda=(\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})\in\widehat{\GL}_{n}
,
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\lambda}
is a connected normal crystal with unique highest weight element
H_{n}^{\lambda}
and lowest weight element
L_{n}^{\lambda}
.

Proof.

First observe
(\mathbb{B}_{n}^{*})^{\otimes k_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{n}^{\otimes k_{2}}
is a normal crystal since
\mathbb{B}_{n}
and
\mathbb{B}_{n}^{*}
are normal, and normality is preserved under tensor product,
[
BumpSchilling2017
, Theorem 5.20]
. Hence,
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}_{n}
is a normal
\GL_{n}
-crystal since we constructed it as a subcrystal of
(\mathbb{B}_{n}^{*})^{\otimes k_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{n}^{\otimes k_{2}}
. Further, by
[
STEMBRIDGE1987
, Proposition 2.4]
, the character of
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}_{n}
equals the character of the irreducible
\GL_{n}
representation with highest weight
\lambda
. Since the character theory of normal crystals and finite dimensional representations of
\GL_{n}
is the same,
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}_{n}
must be the unique connected normal crystal with highest weight
\lambda=\wt(H^{\lambda}_{n})
and lowest weight
w_{0}(\lambda)=\wt(L^{\lambda}_{n})
.
∎

The definition of plactic equivalence was given for normal
\GL_{n}
-crystals, so applies to rational
\GL_{n}
-crystals. Let
\lambda,\mu,\nu\in\widehat{\GL}_{n}
, i.e. rational
\GL_{n}
representations are allowed, and set

\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\GL_{n})\coloneq\{T\in\mathcal{B}^{\nu}_{n}:L^{\mu}_{n}\otimes T\equiv L^{\lambda}_{n}\}.

We extend the notation for Littlewood-Richardson coefficients to rational
\GL_{n}
representations, setting
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}\coloneqq|\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\GL_{n})|
.

Proposition 4.39
.

\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\GL_{n})=\{T\in\mathcal{B}^{\nu}_{n}:\mu_{0}\geq\varphi(T)^{*}\text{ and }\wt(L^{\mu}_{n})+\wt(T)=\wt(L^{\lambda}_{n})\}.

Proof.

This follows identically to the proof of Proposition
4.27
(2).
∎

Proposition 4.40
.

For
[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]\in\widehat{\GL}_{n}^{2}
with
\lambda_{i}=(\lambda_{i}^{+},\lambda_{i}^{-})
, and
\nu=(\nu^{+},\nu^{-})\in\widehat{\GL}_{n}
,

b^{[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]}_{\nu}=|\LC^{\nu}_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}(\GL_{n})|=c^{\nu}_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}.

Proof.

\Res^{\GL_{n}^{2}}_{\GL_{n}}\pi^{[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]}_{\GL_{n}^{2}}=\pi^{\lambda_{1}}_{\GL_{n}}\otimes\pi^{\lambda_{2}}_{\GL_{n}}
and the result follows from the correspondence between normal
\GL_{n}
-crystals and finite dimensional
\GL_{n}
representations.
∎

4.8.
Some symmetries

We continue to work with rational
\GL_{n}
representations in this section. By the correspondence between normal
\GL_{n}
-crystals and finite dimensional
\GL_{n}
representations,

c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}=\dim\Hom_{\GL_{n}}(\pi^{\lambda}_{\GL_{n}},\pi^{\mu}_{\GL_{n}}\otimes\pi^{\nu}_{\GL_{n}}).

Notice
\dim\Hom_{\GL_{n}}(\pi^{\lambda}_{\GL_{n}},\pi^{\mu}_{\GL_{n}}\otimes\pi^{\nu}_{\GL_{n}})
is equal to the dimension of the
\GL_{n}
invariants in a triple product,

\dim\bigg((\pi^{\lambda}_{\GL_{n}})^{*}\otimes\pi^{\mu}_{\GL_{n}}\otimes\pi^{\nu}_{\GL_{n}}\bigg)^{\GL_{n}}.

This reveals many symmetries in the coefficients themselves, see
[
BerensteinZelevinsky1992
]
. In particular,
S_{3}
acts by permuting the three irreducible representations, yielding:

c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}=c^{\lambda}_{\nu\mu}=c^{\mu^{*}}_{\lambda^{*}\nu}=c^{\nu^{*}}_{\mu\lambda^{*}}=c^{\mu^{*}}_{\nu\lambda^{*}}=c^{\nu^{*}}_{\lambda^{*}\mu}.

Taking the dual of the triple product preserves the multiplicity of invariants. Hence, the following coefficients are also equal to
c^{\lambda}_{\mu,\nu}
:

c^{\lambda*}_{\mu^{*}\nu^{*}}=c^{\lambda^{*}}_{\nu^{*}\mu^{*}}=c^{\mu}_{\lambda\nu^{*}}=c^{\nu}_{\mu^{*}\lambda}=c^{\mu}_{\nu^{*}\lambda}=c^{\nu}_{\lambda\mu^{*}}.

We will make use of these symmetries to establish the graded multiplicity formula for
(\GL_{n}^{2},\GL_{n})
.

5.
Branching rules

In this section we develop generalizations of the Littlewood restriction rules: combinatorial rules for computing
\mult(\pi^{\nu}_{\Or_{n}},\pi^{\lambda}_{\GL_{n}})
and
\mult(\pi^{\nu}_{\Sp_{2n}},\pi^{\lambda}_{\GL_{2n}})
where
\pi^{\lambda}_{\GL_{n}}
is a polynomial
\GL_{n}
representation. We focus on lowest weight companion tableaux as defined in Section
4.6
since this makes the connection with the familiar
K
-tableaux from Definition
2.5
most natural. Clearly these results can be translated into various other equivalent statements via the maps
S
,
S^{\pi}
, and
\phi
from Section
4.6
. We will state the rules along with examples and corollaries next. We hope the examples illustrate that this rule is computationally no more difficult than the classical Littlewood restriction rules. Proofs, which rely heavily on
[
Kwon2018
,
KwonJang2021
,
LecouveyLenart2020
]
, are in the following two subsections.

To state the rules, we define two variants of the Littlewood-Richardson coefficient.

Definition 5.1
.

(1)

Let
\lambda,\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2n}
and define
\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Sp_{2n})\coloneq\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\GL_{2n})\cap\mathcal{T}_{\Sp_{2n}}^{\nu,H}
. Call elements of this set
symplectic companion tableaux
. Define
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Sp_{2n})\coloneq|\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Sp_{2n})|
and call this a
symplectic Littlewood-Richardson coefficient
.

(2)

Let
\lambda,\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
and define
\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Or_{n})\coloneq\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\GL_{n})\cap\mathcal{T}_{\Or_{n}}^{\nu}
. Call elements of this set
orthogonal companion tableaux
. Define
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Or_{n})\coloneq|\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Or_{n})|
and call this an
orthogonal Littlewood-Richardson coefficient
.

\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Sp_{2n})
is just the set of
\Sp_{2n}
-tableaux
T
on
\nu
such that
L_{2n}^{\mu}\otimes T\equiv L_{2n}^{\lambda}
. Similarly,
\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Or_{n})
is the set of
\Or_{n}
-tableaux
T
on
\nu
such that
L_{n}^{\mu}\otimes T\equiv L_{n}^{\lambda}
.

We can also characterize these modified Littlewood-Richardson coefficients in terms of Littlewood-Richardson tableaux. For a semistandard skew-tableau
T
, we say a box
b
in
T
is
\Or_{n}
-highlighted
if the entry
i
in
b
is the first or second occurrence of
i
when
w(T)
is read right to left. Similarly, a box is
\Sp_{2n}
-highlighted
if the entry
i
in
b
is the first occurrence of
i
when
w(T)
is read right to left.

Definition 5.2
.

(1)

\HL^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{\mu^{\pi}\nu}(\Sp_{2n})\coloneq\{T\in\HL^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{\mu^{\pi}\nu}(\GL_{n}):\#\{\text{$\Sp_{2n}$-highlighted boxes in the top $2i$ rows}\}\leq i\text{ for all }i\}
and call the elements of this set
symplectic Littlewood-Richardson tableaux
.

(2)

\HL^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{\mu^{\pi}\nu}(\Or_{n})\coloneq\{T\in\HL^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{\mu^{\pi}\nu}(\GL_{n}):\#\{\text{$\Or_{n}$-highlighted boxes in the top $i$ rows}\}\leq i\text{ for all }i\}
and call the elements of this set
orthogonal Littlewood-Richardson tableaux
.

The following lemma says that
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Sp_{2n})
and
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Or_{n})
can be computed via companion tableaux or LR tableaux, just like
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}
.

Lemma 5.3
.

|\LC_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\Sp_{2n})|=|\HL^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{\mu^{\pi}\nu}(\Sp_{2n})|,

|\LC_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\Or_{n})|=|\HL^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{\mu^{\pi}\nu}(\Or_{n})|.

Proof.

T
is an
\Or_{n}
-tableaux if and only if

\#\{\text{boxes in first two columns of $T$ with entry $\leq i$}\}\leq i.

Now by the definition of
\phi:\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\GL_{n})\to\HL^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{\mu^{\pi}\nu}(\GL_{n})
, the highlighted boxes of
\phi(T)
correspond to the boxes coming from the first two columns of
T
. The entries of
T
with values
\leq i
are mapped to rows
\leq i
in
\phi(T)
. So the number of boxes in the first two columns of
T
with entry
\leq i
is equal to the number of highlighted boxes in the first
i
rows of
\phi(T)
. The lemma follows.

Similarly
T
is an
\Sp_{2n}
-tableaux if and only if

\#\{\text{boxes in first column of $T$ with entry $\leq 2i$}\}\leq i.

The
\Sp_{2n}
-highlighted boxes of
\phi(T)
correspond to the boxes coming from the first column of
T
. The entries of
T
with values
\leq 2i
are mapped to rows
\leq 2i
in
\phi(T)
. So the number of boxes in the first column of
T
with entry
\leq 2i
is equal to the number of highlighted boxes in the first
2i
rows of
\phi(T)
. The lemma follows.
∎

Next, we give a few examples illustrating the computation of
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Or_{n})
and
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Sp_{2n})
. We see through the examples that for
n
sufficiently large,
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Or_{n})=c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Sp_{2n})=c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}
.

Example 5.4
.

Consider
(\GL_{4},\Or_{4})
. Suppose we would like to compute
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Or_{4})
for
\lambda=(7,3,3,3),\mu=(2,2,2,0)
, and
\nu=(5,3,1,1)
. We start by determining
\HL^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{\mu^{\pi}\nu}(\GL_{4})
, the set of LR tableaux on
\lambda^{\pi}/\mu^{\pi}
with filling
\nu
. There is only one such tableau, displayed on the left below. Its companion tableau is displayed next to it on the right. The companion tableau is in
\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\GL_{4})
.

*(green) 1

*(green) 1

1

1

*(black)

*(black)

2

2

*(black)

*(black)

3

3

1

4

1

4

2

4

3

4

Next, we determine which members of
\HL^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{\mu^{\pi}\nu}(\GL_{4})
are in
\HL^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{\mu^{\pi}\nu}(\Or_{4})
. Equivalently, we could determine which members of
\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\GL_{4})
are in
\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Or_{4})
. On the LR tableau side, this amounts to checking the condition on highlighted boxes. The
\Or_{4}
-highlighted boxes are colored in the example above. There are two highlighted boxes in the first row, so this LR tableau is excluded. The highlighted boxes in the LR tableau correspond to the boxes in the first two columns of the companion tableau. On the companion tableau side, we see the companion tableau is not an
\Or_{4}
-tableau, so it is excluded.

To get a sense of how these modified LR coefficients lead to a result that stabilizes to the classical Littlewood restriction rule, consider
(\GL_{5},\Or_{5})
. We present the relevant tableaux below.

The tableau in
\HL^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{\mu^{\pi}\nu}(\GL_{5})
is the same as the
n=4
case, except the bottom row is now 5. On the companion side, this has the affect of adding one to every entry: recall, the entries in row
i
of the companion tableau come from the rows containing entry
i
in the LR tableau. For example, the 1’s in the LR tableau occur (written with multiplicity) in rows
\{2,2,2,5,5\}
when
n=5
versus rows
\{1,1,1,4,4\}
when
n=4
. Hence, the entries in row 1 of the companion tableau are
22255
when
n=5
versus
11144
when
n=4
.

This tableau still does not contribute to
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Or_{5})
. On the LR side, the problem does not occur until row 5: we see six highlighted entries in the first five rows. Similarly, on the companion side, we are okay until
i=5
. The support of
[5]
is not an
\Or_{5}
-tableau (For
i<5
, the support of
[i]
is an
\Or_{i}
tableau).

Finally, we reach the stable situation where this tableau is counted. On the left, the number of highlighted boxes in the first

i
. On the right, the companion tableau is an
\Or_{6}
-tableau. For
n>6
, it is easy to see this tableau continues to be counted when computing
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Or_{n})
. From the above, we see
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Or_{n})=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }n<6,\\
1&\text{if }n\geq 6,\\
\end{cases}
and
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Or_{n})=c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}
when
n\geq 6
.

Notice we add 2 to all entries in the companion tableau (coming from the 2 additional empty rows added above the

and it is easy to see it will be counted for all
\Sp_{2n}
with

.

For us, the importance of these modified Littlewood-Richardson coefficients is the following result.

Theorem 5.6
.

(1)

For
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{2n}
,
\nu\in\widehat{\Sp}_{2n}
,

\mult(\pi^{\nu}_{\Sp_{2n}},\pi^{\lambda}_{\GL_{2n}})=\sum_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{(1,1)}_{2n}}c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Sp_{2n}).

(2)

For
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\nu\in\widehat{\Or}_{n}
,

\mult(\pi^{\nu}_{\Or_{n}},\pi^{\lambda}_{\GL_{n}})=\sum_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{n}^{(2)}}c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Or_{n}).

Notice, although we follow the convention of displaying this as a sum over an infinite set,
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{(2)}
or
\mathcal{P}_{2n}^{(1,1)}
, only finitely many terms actually contribute since
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}=0
unless
\mu
is contained in
\lambda
and
|\lambda|-|\mu|=|\nu|
. Next, we give a couple of examples using Theorem
5.6
.

Example 5.7
.

We compute the decomposition of the
\GL_{n}
irreducible representation
\lambda=(2,2,0,\dots,0)
when restricted to
\Or_{n}
. This decomposition depends on
n
, but as we show, stabilizes for
n\geq 4
. Notice, for all but three
\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{(2)}_{n}
,
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}
is automatically zero regardless of
\nu
because
\mu\nsubseteq\lambda
. We only need to check
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}
for
\mu=(0,0,\dots,0),(2,0,\dots,0),(2,2,0,\dots,0)
. The easiest way to approach this is to find all LR tableaux on
\lambda^{\pi}/\mu^{\pi}
for
\mu=(0,0,\dots,0),(2,0,\dots,0),(2,2,0,\dots,0)
and then see which satisfy the condition on
\Or_{n}
-highlighted boxes.

n=2
, the LR tableaux are:

is just the weight of the skew-tableau. In this example
\nu=(2,2),(2,0),(0,0)
in order from left to right. Observe that the first two skew-tableaux violate the condition on highlighted boxes: there are two highlighted boxes in the first row. The final skew-tableau vacuously satisfies the condition on highlighted boxes. Hence
\pi^{(2,2)}_{\GL_{2}}|_{\Or_{2}}=\pi_{\Or_{2}}^{(0,0)}
which is expected as
\pi^{(2,2)}_{\GL_{2}}=\det^{2}
and the square of the determinant is trivial on
\Or_{2}
.

The second tableau is allowed, but the first still is not since there are 4 highlighted boxes in first 3 rows. Hence,
\pi^{(2,2,0)}_{\GL_{3}}|_{\Or_{3}}=\pi^{(2,0,0)}_{\Or_{3}}\oplus\pi_{\Or_{3}}^{(0,0,0)}
.

n=4

width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont\cr}}\kern 1035.4pt},\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{black}\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$4$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont\cr}}\kern 1035.4pt}.

All tableaux are allowed. Hence,
\pi^{(2,2,0,0)}_{\GL_{4}}|_{\Or_{4}}=\pi^{(2,2,0,0)}_{\Or_{4}}\oplus\pi^{(2,0,0,0)}_{\Or_{4}}\oplus\pi_{\Or_{4}}^{(0,0,0,0)}
. It is also clear that at
n=4
the decomposition stabilizes, that is for all
n\geq 4
we have
\pi^{(2,2,0,\dots,0)}_{\GL_{n}}|_{\Or_{n}}=\pi^{(2,2,0,\dots,0)}_{\Or_{n}}\oplus\pi^{(2,0,\dots,0)}_{\Or_{n}}\oplus\pi_{\Or_{n}}^{(0,\dots,0)}
. As discussed in
[
GoodmanWallach2009
, Proposition 10.3.6 and 10.3.7]
this is the decomposition of the space of curvature tensors on
\C^{n}
relative to the natural action of
\Or_{n}
into Ricci curvature tensors, Weyl conformal curvature tensors, and scalar curvature tensors respectively.

Example 5.8
.

Next, we compute the decomposition of the
\GL_{2n}
irreducible representation
\lambda=(2,2,0,\dots,0)
when restricted to
\Sp_{2n}
. This is very similar to the orthogonal example so we move faster.

For
(\GL_{2},\Sp_{2})
, the relevant Littlewood-Richardson tableaux are:

Only the skew-diagram corresponding to
\mu=(2,2)
is allowed. Hence
\pi^{(2,2)}_{\GL_{2}}|_{\Sp_{2}}=\pi^{(0)}_{\Sp_{2}}
which is expected as the
\pi^{(2,2)}_{\GL_{2}}=\det^{2}
and the square of the determinant is trivial on
\Sp_{2}
.

(\GL_{4},\Sp_{4})
, we have:

0.39993pt}\nullfont&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$2$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{black}\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{black}\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$3$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{black}\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{black}\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$4$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont\cr}}\kern 1035.4pt},

all of which are allowed, so
\pi^{(2,2,0,0)}_{\GL_{4}}|_{\Sp_{4}}=\pi^{(2,2)}_{\Sp_{4}}\oplus\pi^{(1,1)}_{\Sp_{4}}\oplus\pi_{\Sp_{4}}^{(0,0)}
. The decomposition stabilizes at
n=2
, i.e. this decomposition holds for all
(\GL_{2n},\Sp_{2n})
with
n\geq 2
.

Next we formalize the stability condition which we observed in the examples above.

Corollary 5.9

(Littlewood Restriction Rules)
.

(1)

For
\ell(\lambda)\leq n
,

\mult(\pi^{\nu}_{\Sp_{2n}},\pi^{\lambda}_{\GL_{2n}})=\sum_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2n}^{(1,1)}}c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}.

(2)

For
\ell(\lambda)\leq n/2
,

\mult(\pi^{\nu}_{\Or_{n}},\pi^{\lambda}_{\GL_{n}})=\sum_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{n}^{(2)}}c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}.

Proof.

(1) For
\Sp_{2n}
, if
\ell(\lambda)\leq n
, then any LR tableau
T\in\LR^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{\mu^{\pi},\nu}(\GL_{2n})
has
\geq n
empty rows above it. Notice there can be at most one
\Sp_{2n}
-highlighted box in each nonempty row thanks to the Yamanouchi condition: the first row in which
i
occurs cannot contain
i+1
. Hence,

\#\{\text{highlighted boxes in first }2i+n\text{ rows}\}\leq 2i.

The
\Sp_{2n}
condition stipulates that

\#\{\text{highlighted boxes in first }2i+n\text{ rows}\}\leq i+n/2.

Thus, the
\Sp_{2n}
condition is satisfied for all
i\leq n/2
, i.e. for all rows up to row
2n
, the final row. Hence, no elements of
\LR^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{\mu^{\pi}\nu}(\GL_{2n})
are excluded,
\LR^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{\mu^{\pi}\nu}(\GL_{2n})=\LR^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{\mu^{\pi}\nu}(\Sp_{2n})
. The result follows from Theorem
5.6
.

(2) The
\Or_{n}
situation is similar. If
\ell(\lambda)\leq n/2
, then any LR tableau
T\in\LR^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{\mu^{\pi},\nu}(\GL_{n})
has
\geq n/2
empty rows above it. Notice there can be at most
2i
highlighted boxes in the first
i
nonempty rows because of the Yamanouchi condition: the first row
r
in which
i
occurs must be
\geq
row
i
. The nonempty rows above and including
r
can only contain highlighted boxes with entries
\leq i
of which there are at most
2i
. Hence,

\#\{\text{highlighted boxes in first }i+n/2\text{ rows}\}\leq 2i.

The
\Or_{n}
condition stipulates that

\#\{\text{highlighted boxes in first }i+n/2\text{ rows}\}\leq i+n/2.

Thus, the
\Or_{n}
condition is satisfied for
i\leq n/2
, i.e. for all rows up to row
n
, the final row. Hence
\LR^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{\mu^{\pi}\nu}(\GL_{n})=\LR^{\lambda^{\pi}}_{\mu^{\pi}\nu}(\Or_{n})
and the result follows from Theorem
5.6
.
∎

5.1.
Proof of the symplectic group rule

We prove

\mult(\pi^{\nu}_{\Sp_{2n}},\pi^{\lambda}_{\GL_{2n}})=\sum_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{(1,1)}_{2n}}|\LC_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\Sp_{2n})|.

Proof.

In
[
LecouveyLenart2020
]
, a subset
\overline{\HC}^{\lambda^{\prime}}_{\nu^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}(\GL_{n})
of
\HC^{\lambda^{\prime}}_{\nu^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}(\GL_{n})
is defined and
\overline{c}^{\lambda}_{\nu\mu}\coloneq|\overline{\HC}^{\lambda^{\prime}}_{\nu^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}(\GL_{n})|
, (in
[
LecouveyLenart2020
]
these sets are denoted
\overline{{\LR}}^{\lambda^{\prime}}_{\nu^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}}
and
\LR^{\lambda^{\prime}}_{\nu^{\prime},\mu}
).
[
LecouveyLenart2020
, Corollary 6.9]
gives us

(1)

\mult(\pi^{\nu}_{\Sp_{2n}},\pi^{\lambda}_{\GL_{2n}})=\sum_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{(1,1)}_{2n}}\bar{c}^{\lambda}_{\nu\mu}.

Now consider
b\in\HC^{\lambda}_{\nu\mu}(\GL_{2n})
with
\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{(1,1)}_{2n}
. Let
\sigma_{\mu}
be the bijection
\HC^{\lambda}_{\nu\mu}(\GL_{2n})\to\HC^{\lambda^{\prime}}_{\nu^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}(\GL_{2n})
as in
[
LecouveyLenart2020
]
and
S:\mathcal{B}^{\nu}_{n}\to\mathcal{B}^{\nu}_{n}
the Schützenberger involution. By
[
LecouveyLenart2020
, Lemma 6.11]
,
\sigma_{\mu}(b)\in\overline{{\HC}}^{\lambda^{\prime}}_{\nu^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}(\GL_{2n})
if and only if
S(b)
has entries in row
i
at least
2i-1
for
i=1,\dots,n
. As
S
gives a bijection between
\HC^{\lambda}_{\nu\mu}(\GL_{2n})
and
\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\GL_{2n})
(Theorem
4.33
above), we see the set of
\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\GL_{2n})
with entries in row
i
at least
2i-1
for
i=1,\dots,n
has cardinality
\bar{c}_{\nu,\mu}^{\lambda}
. By the discussion after Definition
2.5
,
\mathcal{T}^{\nu,H}_{\Sp_{2n}}
can be characterized as the set of the set of tableaux on
\nu
with entries in row
i
at least
2i-1
for
i=1,\dots,n
. Since
\LC_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\Sp_{2n})=\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\GL_{2n})\cap\mathcal{T}^{\nu,H}_{\Sp_{2n}}
,
|\LC_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\Sp_{2n})|=\bar{c}^{\lambda}_{\nu\mu}
and the theorem follows from (
1
).

∎

5.2.
Proof of the orthogonal group rule

We prove

\mult(\pi^{\nu}_{\Or_{n}},\pi^{\lambda}_{\GL_{n}})=\sum_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{(2)}_{n}}|\LC_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\Or_{n})|.

Let
\nu=(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{n})
be a partition and
\nu^{\prime}=(\nu_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\nu^{\prime}_{l})
be the conjugate partition. We start with the following definition of Jang and Kwon,

Definition 5.10
.

[
KwonJang2021
, Definition 4.6]

Let
T\in\SST^{\nu^{\pi}}_{n}
and if
n-2\nu_{1}^{\prime}<0
, set r =
n-\nu_{1}^{\prime}
. Otherwise,
r=\nu_{1}^{\prime}
. For
1\leq i\leq\nu_{1}^{\prime}
and
1\leq j\leq\nu_{2}^{\prime}
, let

•

\sigma_{i}=
the
i
th entry in the rightmost column of
T
from the bottom,

•

\tau_{j}=
the
j
th entry in the second rightmost column of
T
from the bottom,

•

m_{i}=\begin{cases}\min\{n+1-\sigma_{i},2i-1\}&\text{if $1\leq i\leq r$,}\\
\min\{n+1-\sigma_{i},n+i-\nu_{1}^{\prime}\}&\text{if $r<i\leq\nu^{\prime}_{1}$}\end{cases}

•

n_{j}=
the
j
th smallest number in
\{j+1,\dots,n\}/\penalty\{m_{j+1},\dots,m_{\nu_{1}^{\prime}}\}
.

Say
T
is a
Jang-Kwon orthogonal tableau
or a
JK tableau
if

\tau_{j}\leq n+1-n_{j}\quad\text{ for }\;1\leq j\leq\nu_{2}^{\prime}.

Let
\JK_{n}^{\nu^{\pi}}
be the set of JK tableaux on shape
\nu^{\pi}
with elements in alphabet
[n]
and
\JK^{\lambda}_{\nu^{\pi}\mu}=\JK_{n}^{\nu^{\pi}}\cap\HC^{\lambda}_{\nu^{\pi}\mu}(\GL_{n})
.

Jang and Kwon denote the set
\JK^{\lambda}_{\nu^{\pi}\mu}
by
\underline{\text{LR}}^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}
. They derive the following branching formula.

Theorem 5.11
.

[
KwonJang2021
, Theorem 4.17]

For
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
and
\nu\in\widehat{\Or}_{n}
,

\mult(\pi^{\nu}_{\Or_{n}},\pi^{\lambda}_{\GL_{n}})=\sum_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{(2)}_{n}}|\JK^{\lambda}_{\nu^{\pi}\mu}|.

In light of this result, it suffices to exhibit a bijection between
\JK_{\nu^{\pi}\mu}^{\lambda}
and
\LC_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\Or_{n})
. As in the
\Sp_{2n}
case, the map providing this bijection is the Schützenberger involution: we claim
S^{\pi}:\mathcal{B}^{\nu^{\pi}}_{n}\to\mathcal{B}^{\nu}_{n}
restricts to a bijection between
\JK_{\nu^{\pi}\mu}^{\lambda}
and
\LC_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\Or_{n})
. We already know
S^{\pi}
restricts to a bijection between
\HC_{\nu^{\pi}\mu}^{\lambda}(\GL_{n})
and
\LC_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\GL_{n})
by Theorem
4.33
. Hence, it suffices to show that
S^{\pi}
restricts to a bijection between
\JK_{n}^{\nu^{\pi}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\Or_{n}}^{\nu}
since then:

\displaystyle S^{\pi}(\JK^{\lambda}_{\nu^{\pi}\mu})

\displaystyle=S^{\pi}(\JK^{\nu^{\pi}}_{n}\cap\HC^{\lambda}_{\nu^{\pi}\mu}(\GL_{n})),

\displaystyle=S^{\pi}(\JK^{\nu^{\pi}}_{n})\cap S^{\pi}(\HC^{\lambda}_{\nu^{\pi}\mu}(\GL_{n})),

\displaystyle=T^{\nu}_{\Or_{n}}\cap\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Or_{n}),

\displaystyle=\LC^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(\Or_{n}).

To this end, we begin by applying
S^{\pi}
to
\JK_{n}^{\nu^{\pi}}
and characterizing the image in
\SST_{n}^{\nu}
. This is simply a matter of keeping track of how the conditions of Definition
5.10
transform under
S^{\pi}
. Recall,
S^{\pi}
rotates
\nu^{\pi}
by
180^{\circ}
so it becomes
\nu
and sends the entry
i\mapsto n+1-i
. It is easy to check that Definition
5.10
becomes,

Definition 5.12
.

Let
T\in\SST^{\nu}_{n}
and if
\nu_{1}^{\prime}>n/2
, set r =
n-\nu_{1}^{\prime}
. Otherwise,
r=\nu_{1}^{\prime}
. For
1\leq i\leq\nu_{1}^{\prime}
and
1\leq j\leq\nu_{2}^{\prime}
, let

•

\sigma_{i}=
the
i
th entry in the leftmost column of
T
from the top,

•

\tau_{j}=
the
j
th entry in the second leftmost column of
T
from the top,

•

m_{i}=\begin{cases}\min\{\sigma_{i},2i-1\}&\text{if $1\leq i\leq r$,}\\
\min\{\sigma_{i},r+i\}&\text{if $r<i\leq\nu^{\prime}_{1}$}\end{cases}

•

n_{j}=
the
j
th smallest number in
\{j+1,\dots,n\}/\penalty\{m_{j+1},\dots,m_{\nu_{1}^{\prime}}\}
.

Say
T
is a
rotated Jang-Kwon orthogonal tableau
or a
RJK tableau
if

\tau_{j}\geq n_{j}\quad\text{ for }\;1\leq j\leq\nu_{2}^{\prime}.

Let
\RJK_{n}^{\nu}
be the set of RJK tableaux on shape
\nu
with elements in the alphabet
[n]
.

By construction,
S^{\pi}
restricts to a bijection
\JK_{n}^{\nu^{\pi}}\to\RJK_{n}^{\nu}
. To finish the proof, we need to show
\RJK_{n}^{\nu}=\mathcal{T}_{\Or_{n}}^{\nu}
. Before doing that, we simplify the characterization of
\RJK
tableaux a little bit.

Definition 5.13
.

Let
T\in\SST^{\nu}_{n}
. For
1\leq i\leq\nu_{1}^{\prime}
and
1\leq j\leq\nu_{2}^{\prime}
, let

•

\sigma_{i}=
the
i
th entry in the leftmost column of
T
from the top,

•

\tau_{j}=
the
j
th entry in the second leftmost column of
T
from the top,

•

n_{j}=
the
j
th smallest number in
\{j+1,\dots,n\}/\penalty\{\sigma_{j+1},\dots,\sigma_{\nu_{1}^{\prime}}\}
.

Say
T
is a
rotated Jang-Kwon orthogonal tableau
or a
RJK tableau
if

\tau_{j}\geq n_{j}\quad\text{ for }\;1\leq j\leq\nu_{2}^{\prime}.

Let
\RJK_{n}^{\nu}
be the set of RJK tableaux on shape
\nu
with elements in alphabet
[n]
.

Lemma 5.14
.

The two definitions of RJK tableaux are equivalent.

Proof.

We show that the computation of
m_{i}
in the first definition of RJK tableaux is unnecessary: we can just set
m_{i}=\sigma_{i}
. We do this in two steps.

First, if
\nu_{1}^{\prime}>n/2
and
r<i\leq\nu_{1}^{\prime}
, then we claim
\sigma_{i}<r+i
. To see this, for any
i>r
, write
i=\nu_{1}^{\prime}-j
where
j
is the number of boxes in the first column below
\sigma_{i}
and notice
r+i=(n-\nu_{1}^{\prime})+(\nu_{1}^{\prime}-j)=n-j
. Since columns are strictly increasing, there are
j
terms of
[n]
that are greater than
\sigma_{i}
, hence
\sigma_{i}<n-j=r+i
as claimed. Since
2i-1\geq r+i
for all
i>r
, there is no harm in replacing
\min\{\sigma_{i},r+i\}
with
\min\{\sigma_{i},2i-1\}
. Hence, we can take
m_{i}=\min\{\sigma_{i},2i-1\}
for all
i
. Notice
m_{i}<m_{i+1}
:
\sigma_{i}<\sigma_{i+1}
since columns are strictly increasing and
2i-1<2i+1
.

Second, assume
2i-1<\sigma_{i}
so that
m_{i}=2i-1
. We claim
n_{j}=
the
j^{\text{th}}
smallest number in
\{j+1,\dots,n\}/\penalty\{m_{j+1},\dots,\widehat{m_{i}},\dots,m_{\nu_{1}^{\prime}}\}
, where
\widehat{m_{i}}
means exclude
m_{i}
, i.e. for all
j
, the computation of
n_{j}
is not impacted by excluding
m_{i}
. Recall the
n_{j}
are the actual constraints on the entries in the second column. If the
n_{j}
are not impacted by excluding
m_{i}
, then the characterization of RJK tableaux is not impacted by excluding
m_{i}
.

If
j\geq i
,
m_{i}\notin\{m_{j+1},\dots,m_{\nu_{1}^{\prime}}\}
so it does not impact
n_{j}
. Suppose
j<i
and write
i=j+k
. The
j
th smallest number in
\{j+1,\dots,n\}
is
2j
. By assumption,
m_{i}=2i-1=2j+2k-1>2j
, so if no
m_{j+1},\dots,m_{j+k-1}
impacts the computation of
n_{j}
, i.e. all
m_{j+\delta}
are greater than
2j
, then
m_{i}
does not impact the computation either (it is too big to involve the
j^{\text{th}}
term of
\{j+1,\dots,n\}
- it is the
j+2k-1
term). The closest we can get to
m_{i}
impacting the computation of
n_{j}
is if all
m_{j+1},\dots,m_{j+k-1}
are less than
2j
. Since
m_{i}<m_{i+1}
, we only need to consider
j+\delta<i
. If
m_{i}
does not impact
n_{j}
, then neither does any
m_{j+\delta}
for
j+\delta>i
. If all
m_{j+1},\dots,m_{j+k-1}
are less than
2j
, the
j^{\text{th}}
smallest term of
\{j+1,\dots,n\}/\penalty\{m_{j+1},\dots,m_{j+k-1}\}
is
2j+k-1
which is less than
m_{i}=2j+2k
. Hence,
n_{j}=
the
j^{\text{th}}
smallest number in
\{j+1,\dots,n\}/\penalty\{m_{j+1},\dots,\widehat{m_{i}},\dots,m_{\nu_{1}^{\prime}}\}
as claimed. By assumption,
\sigma_{i}>m_{i}
so exchanging
\sigma_{i}
with
m_{i}
will not impact the computation either. Hence, we arrive at the same set of tableaux if we set
m_{i}=\sigma_{i}
for all
i
.

Notice, the only place
r
is used is in the computation of
m_{i}
, so we can drop
r
from the characterization as well.

∎

The following lemma completes the proof that
S^{\pi}:\JK_{n}^{\nu^{\pi}}\to\mathcal{T}^{\nu}_{\Or_{n}}
is a bijection and hence establishes the
\Or_{n}
branching rule in Theorem
5.6
.

Lemma 5.15
.

\RJK_{n}^{\nu}=\mathcal{T}_{\Or_{n}}^{\nu}
.

Proof.

The characterization of
\mathcal{T}^{\lambda}_{\Or_{n}}
as tableaux
T\in\SST^{\lambda}_{n}
such that
\#\{
boxes in first two columns of
T
with entry
\leq i\}\leq i
for all
i
can be understood as a constraint on the entries in the second column of
T
. Any strictly increasing filling of the first column is possible. We show that restating the
\Or_{n}
-tableau condition as a constraint on the fillings of the second column gives the RJK tableaux.

Let
\sigma_{1}<\dots<\sigma_{\nu_{1}^{\prime}}
be a filling of the first column of
\nu
and let
b_{k,l}
denote the
k^{\text{th}}
box from the top in column
l
, so the entry in
b_{k,1}
is
\sigma_{k}
. For
1\leq j\leq\nu_{2^{\prime}}
, we find the minimum value allowed in
b_{j,2}
, denoted
n_{j}
, such that
\#\{\text{boxes in first two columns of $T$ with entry $\leq n_{j}$}\}\leq n_{j}
. Since we must have
n_{j-1}<n_{j}<n_{j+1}
, the
n_{j}
computations can all be performed independently. In any semistandard filling of
T
, all boxes northwest of
b_{j,2}
have entry less than or equal to the entry in
b_{j,2}
, so there will be at least
2j
entries less than or equal to the entry in
b_{j,k}
. Hence, only considering boxes northwest of
b_{j,2}
, the minimum allowed value in
b_{j,2}
is
2j
. Set
n_{j}=2j
. We will adjust this initial computation of
n_{j}
for entries below
b_{j,1}
in column one. To make the connection with RJK tableaux clear, we chose to compute
2j
as the
j^{\text{th}}
smallest number in
\{j+1,\dots,n\}
. Notice,
\#\{b_{1,1},b_{2,1},\dots,b_{j,1};b_{1,2},\dots,b_{\nu_{2}^{\prime},2}:\text{the entry in $b_{k,l}$ is }\leq n_{j}\}=n_{j}
. We can include all boxes in the second column since any box
b_{l,2}
with
l>j
has entry
>n_{j}
.

Entries below
b_{j,1}
in column 1 can impact
n_{j}
. Moving down column 1, if
\sigma_{j+1}\leq n_{j}
, then
\#\{b_{1,1},b_{2,1},\dots,b_{j+1,1};b_{1,2},\dots,b_{\nu_{2}^{\prime},2}:\text{the entry in $b_{k,l}$ is }\leq n_{j}\}=n_{j}+1
. Hence we must add 1 to
n_{j}
,
n_{j}=2j+1
. We compute this updated
n_{j}
as the
j^{\text{th}}
smallest number in
\{j+1,\dots,n\}/\{\sigma_{j+1}\}
. Note,
\sigma_{j+1}\geq j+1
since the filling of column one is strictly increasing. With

\#\{b_{1,1},b_{2,1},\dots,b_{j+1,1};b_{1,2},\dots,b_{\nu_{2}^{\prime},2}:\text{the entry in $b_{k,l}$ is }\leq n_{j}\}=n_{j}

restored, we continue. Once we reach the bottom of column 1, we arrive at
\#\{
boxes in first two columns of
T
with entry
\leq n_{j}\}\leq n_{j}
where
n_{j}=
the
j^{\text{th}}
smallest number in
\{j+1,\dots,n\}/\penalty\{\sigma_{j+1},\dots,\sigma_{\nu_{1}^{\prime}}\}
.
∎

Applying
S^{\pi}
to
\mathcal{T}^{\nu}_{\Or_{n}}
, we get a simpler characterization of the JK-tableaux,

Corollary 5.16
.

T\in\SST^{\nu^{\pi}}_{n}
is a JK-tableau if and only if

\#\{\text{boxes in right two columns with entry}\geq n+1-i\}\leq i.

6.
Graded multiplicities of
K
-types

Recall from Definition
2.7
,
(T^{\nu}_{K})_{0}
are the tableaux representing a basis for the zero
\mwt
space in
\pi^{\nu}_{K}
and
m^{\nu,0}_{(G,K)}(q)=\mult_{q}(\pi^{\nu}_{K},\mathcal{H})
. The main result of this section is the following theorem,

Theorem 6.1
.

For
(\GL_{n},\Or_{n})
,
(\GL_{n}^{2},\GL_{n})
, and
(\GL_{2n},\Sp_{2n})
,

m^{\nu,0}_{(G,K)}(q)=\sum_{T\in(\mathcal{T}_{K}^{\nu})_{0}}q^{d(T)},

where

(G,K)

d(T)

(\GL_{n},\Or_{n})

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\bigg(|\nu|+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(n-i)\cdot\lceil\varphi_{i}(T)\rceil\bigg)
,

(\GL_{n}^{2},\GL_{n})

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(n-i)\cdot\varphi_{i}(T)
,

(\GL_{2n},\Sp_{2n})

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\bigg(|\nu|+\sum_{i=1}^{2n-1}(n-i)\cdot\varphi_{i}(T)\bigg)
,

and
\lceil-\rceil
means round up to an even integer.

The
(\GL_{n}^{2},\GL_{n})
case is an incarnation of the well-known charge statistic stated in terms of rational
\GL_{n}
-tableaux. As discussed in the introduction,
m^{\nu,0}_{(G,K)}(q)
is equal to the Hodge
K
-character of the spherical principal series representation of
G_{\R}
with infinitesimal character 0. The
\varphi_{i}
can be computed combinatorially via the signature rule as in Examples
4.8
,
4.11
,
4.12
, and
4.36
.

The first subsection below presents explicit computations of a number of examples for
(\GL_{4},\Or_{4})
, with the goal of recomputing the table of
q
-multiplicities found at
[
Willenbring2000
, 80]
. The second subsection determines the graded multiplicity of the unique irreducible representation occurring in degree one of the harmonics. The third subsection contains the proof of Theorem
6.1
.

6.1.
\Or_{4}
Examples

We begin with a number of examples for
\Or_{4}
and tie them to the results found in
[
Willenbring2000
, 80]
. The results in
[
Willenbring2000
,
WallachWillenbring2000
]
are for
(\SL_{4},\Sor_{4})
while the results in this paper are for
(\GL_{4},\Or_{4})
. It is easy to translate our results to the
(\SL_{n},\Sor_{n})
situation by remembering how
\Or_{n}
representations restrict to
\Sor_{n}
. This is an advantage of working with
\Or_{n}
instead of
\Sor_{n}
. In general, it is not clear how to translate results for
(\SL_{n},\Sor_{n})
to results about
(\GL_{n},\Or_{n})
. We also note that the
q
-multiplicities seem to have nicer properties in the
(\GL_{n},\Or_{n})
setting. For example, they appear to be palindromic.

We begin by recalling how representations of
\Or_{4}
restrict to
\Sor_{4}
, see
[
GoodmanWallach2009
, Theorem 5.5.24]
or
[
FultonHarris1991
, Theorem 19.22]
. Write
\Or_{4}\cong\{1,g_{0}\}\ltimes\Sor_{4}
as in
[
GoodmanWallach2009
, 275]
. Let
P_{++}(\Sor_{4})=\{\mu_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\mu_{2}\epsilon_{2}:\mu_{1}\geq|\mu_{2}|\}
be the dominant weights of
\Sor_{4}
. We think of dominant weights as tuples via the identification
\mu_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\mu_{2}\epsilon_{2}\longleftrightarrow(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
. Let
\varpi_{1}=\frac{1}{2}(\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{2})
and
\varpi_{2}=\frac{1}{2}(\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2})
be the fundamental weights for
\sor_{4}
. The element
\g_{0}
acts on the dominant weight lattice by
g_{0}\cdot\epsilon_{1}=\epsilon_{1}
and
g_{0}\cdot\epsilon_{2}=-\epsilon_{2}
. If
\nu\in\widehat{\Or}_{4}
, its
associated partition

\overline{\nu}
is the partition obtained from
\nu
by changing the length of the first column from
\ell(\nu)
to
4-\ell(\nu)
and leaving all other columns unchanged.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
\nu\in\widehat{\Or}_{4}
.

(1)

If
\nu\neq\overline{\nu}
, then
\pi^{\nu}_{\Or_{4}}|_{\Sor_{4}}
is irreducible and
\pi^{\nu}_{\Or_{4}}|_{\Sor_{4}}=\pi^{\overline{\nu}}_{\Or_{4}}|_{\Sor_{4}}
.

(2)

If
\nu=\overline{\nu}
, then
\pi^{\nu}_{\Or_{4}}|_{\Sor_{4}}=\pi^{\nu}_{\Sor_{4}}\oplus\pi^{g_{0}\cdot\nu}_{\Sor_{4}}
.

Notice, case (1) says that the hook shape
\nu=(k,1,1,0)
restricts to the same
\Sor_{4}
representation as
\overline{\nu}=(k,0,0,0)
, namely the representation with highest weight
k\epsilon_{1}=(k,0)
and the trivial representation
(0,0,0,0)
restricts to the same representation as the determinant
(1,1,1,1)
, the trivial representation
(0,0)
. Case (2) says that
\pi^{(k_{1},k_{2},0,0)}_{\Or_{4}}
restricts to
\pi^{(k_{1},k_{2})}_{\Sor_{4}}\oplus\pi^{(k_{1},-k_{2})}_{\Sor_{4}}
.

Observe that
\nu
can only have
M
-weight zero tableaux if
|\nu|
is even since the filling must have an even number of
i
for all
i\in[4]
. We start with the simplest non-trivial example.

Example 6.3
.

Consider
\nu=(2,0,0,0)=\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}

Next, we compute the grading. By the signuature rule,

\varphi_{i}(\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$2$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$2$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt})=\begin{cases}2&\text{if }i=2,\\
0&\text{else.}\end{cases}
